MRS. FRENCH-SHELDON. Mrs. French-Sheldon, one of the few lady Fellows of the Royal Geographical Society, has just started for another short expedition to the West Coast of Africa. Mrs. French-Sheldon is the daughter of the late Colonel Joseph French, • a great mathematician, while her mother is quite celebrated as one of the first women doctors in the States. Men and women of light and learning were wont to frequent her parents' home, and she learnt much in her early years by listening to the brilliant talk that went on around her. She owes her regular education ttiefly to Italy, where her love of music was fullyindulged, and she at one time thought of making it her profession. Of recent years she has dropped it entirely, finding it too exacting a master for one of her wandering propensities. ' The objects of Mrs:. Sheldon's journeys through ' Africa are almost entirely ethnological, and the women of the countries in which she travels exact her chief interest.

Ix one second a telegraphic message would go seven times round the earth. So says Sir Robert Ball.

IT is usually imagined that the' incandescent electric light gives out very little heat. As a matter of fact, only 6 per cent, of its energy goes to make light, while 94 per cent. goes into heat.

Fnom photographs of the spectra of Uranus and Neptune, the outermost of the sun's family of planets, it is ihferred that free hydrogen is abundant, especially on Neptune, and that helium exists on Uranus. Water vapour is indi- cated, and possibly gases yet unknown.

CHARMING COAT AND SHIRT.

A coat and skirt of the more ornate order is shewn in our sketch. It would be a charming 1 costume for afternoon wear during the winter. ! One of the new wine-coloured faced cloths would ! look warm and comfortable, with. facings of black caraCal; and here some odd pieces of good fur ! might be used, such as a cape that has become !, old-fashioned or worn in parts. Or a good quality earacal cloth would be an excellent substitute for real fur, and a good deal less expensive. The ! skirt of this costume is perfectly plain, but very

full at the hem. The coat fits closely in at the back. The upper part of the fronts are cut short, like an Eton coat, and hang loosely over a very deep belt of wine.coloured velvet. This belt is attached to the coat at the sides, and fastens with the "skirts" of the coat in front. The large revers and collar are faced with caracal, and the full sleeves, that reach to just below the elbow, are drafrn into cuffs of the same fur. For the sake of warmth, long, tight-fitting cuffs of velvet are worn; these button on the outside, and look very smart. Tiny velvet-covered but- tons adorn the coat, and a fussy lace cravat gives a dainty finish.

===== 0002601_19051118_art0011.txt | included=true | included =====
THE INFLUENCE OF PERFUME.

The modern woman is possessed by a craze for perfumes. Perhaps, writes " Ambrosia " in the World, it is an indication of degeneracy, since most nice things are signs and tokens that we are individually withering and nationally going to pieces. Anyhow, there is no mistake about the fact that perfumery is a cult with us. and that we pursue it most thoroughly. The art of using scent is mysteriously to suggest it in a dozen different ways. Most men dislike strong perfumes; but they can be strangely in- fluenced and potently attracted by them if they are used with skill. It is only lately that women have learnt how to do this; and it is quite an interesting study now to note the various methods •employed by one's friends faintly to , suggest in themselves or in their rooms some special odour which steals upon the senses of those who come into their presence. I saw it stated the other day that some German doctor declared that bachelors were made by perfumes, or, in other words, that as certain odours will keep certain animals at bay, so strong scents drive men, so to say, from a woman's presence. They themselves are not aware that it is this or that sweet essence which ltecps them from liking certain folk, but it is so. And if this be so, it is high time indeed that the scientific use of scent came to be understood of women, and as artistically employed as cosmetics.

SMART NHWEI-VEILING SHIRT.

Our sketch illustrates a very smart and be- coming shirt of white nun's-veiling. The ar- rangement of the front of the blouse is quite novel, and possesses a chit distinction all its own. A deep, shaped yoke is finished in the middle by a wide, pointed ,tab coming over and buttoned down to an equally wide box-pleat. On either side of the pleat are two wide tucks, starting from the yoke and running to the

bottom of the blouse. The yoke, the box-pleat, and the tucks are all adorned with rows of stitching in some prettily contrasting silk, and with buttons matching in colour the stitching. The sleeves are cut in a piece with the yoke, and are slit up the middle, allowing of the inser- tion of a plain band between the cut edges. The cleeves as well as the cuffs are stitched and but- toned to match the rest of the blouse. LADY COOKS.

The secretary of the Arachne Club, which Mms, among other things, at the training of ladies as domestics, says that the difficulty of obtaining cooks, gentlewomen or otherwise, is greater than ever. "A number of the ladies whom we train as cooks leave us to go home, and do not go out to service at all. Many of our lady cooks will go into fiats, where there is no heavy work, such as carrying coals, but it is very difficult to persuade them to take a :;itua.• tion in a large house."

Boil until the vegctebles are sufficiently soft tc pulp them, press them through a sieve, and re. turn them to the saucepan; add throe pint: more stock, 2oz. of butter, a sessoning of salt white pepper, and two tablespOonfuls of flour, which have been moistened to • thin, smootl

A TEST FOR BACTERIA.

A delicate test for bacterial contamination tins 'been described in an Dalian scientific paper. The reagent used is a solution of alkaline t-Alu- rides. It is found that most living bacteria de- compose a solution ' of potassium telluride with the formation of a black precipitate. Dead bac- teria, or spores not undergoing development, aro without action on such a solution. Thus a sample of water, for example, suspected of bac- terial contamination, might be readily tested by the addition of a little potassium telluride. If bacteria were present the black precipitate would appear.

===== 0002601_19051118_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
FATIGUE AND ACCIDENTS.

A paper by MM. Ibert and Mestre has been published in the Revue Scientiflque, in which the authors endeavour to shew that fatigue is largely responsible for the occurrence of accidenti in in- dustrial undertakings. In 1904 there were 4,352 accidents to workmen in the Departments of the Herault, Aveyron, Lozere, Centel, and Tarn. An analysis of the official returns shews that 118 occurred between 1 a.m. and 5 a.m., and 151 be- tween 8 p.m. and midnight. During the rest of the day the numbers were grouped as follows: Between 5 and 6, 110; 6 and 7, 232; 7 and 8, 305; 8 and 9, 340: 9 and 10, 478; 10 and 11, 292; 11 and 12, 50; 12 and 1, 132; 1 and 2, 310; 2 and 3, 421: 3 and 4, 588; 4 and 51 513; 5 and 6, 254; 6 and 7, 58.

===== 0002601_19051118_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
SYNTHETIC ALCOHOL.

It was at one time very commonly supposed that organic chemical compounds, such tis sugar, starch, ttc., could only be built up by plant or animal growth. A large number of such organic compounds, however, has now been synthesised from inorganic material. Among these may be mentioned alcohol, which hp been thus pro- duced on more than one ocB.sion. Some experi- ments for thus making it which have been carried out at gutcum are • apparently with a iew to its manufacture on a commercial scale. The first stage is the manufacture of a carbide called " ethylogeno " by heating together a mix- ture of coke, lime, and various metallic oxides in an electric furnace. The action of sulphuric acid on this gives sulphonic acid, which, being distilled with water, yields alcohol.

===== 0002601_19051118_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
RADIO-ACTIVE ELEMENTS.

The investigations of Ramsay and others indi- cate that helium is produced by the disinte,rra- tion of radium. After examining a number of analyses of minerals containing those radio- active elements, uranium and thorium, Mr. Ber- tram Boltwood suggests that many of the other elements found in these minerals aro also dis- integration products. These elements include lead, barium, bismuth, the rare earths, argon, and hydrogen, and it is thought that they are the products of disintegration of the radio- active elements. And the question is further raised whether these elements as they exist in Nature are not all disintegration products. This theory pushed a little further would suggest, says the Globe, that the earth. was originally altogether made up, of radio-active elements, such as radium, thorium, and uranium.

===== 0002601_19051118_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
A FIREMAN'S HOOD.

A Californian, named Chapin, has invented an ingenious respiratory apparatus for firemen. It consists of a hood lined with oiled silk to cover the head, and an air cylinder, which is strapped to the back. This cylinder is divided into three compartments, and contains compressed air sufficient to last for an hour. The air is con- ducted by a tube to the head-piece, and tho ex- haled air misses through a valve in front. The cylinder is so arranged that the supply of air cannot be used too quickly, as would be apt to be the case under the influence of fright. In order to test the invention a man equipped with the apparatus entered a room filled with the fumes of burning sulphur. He remained work- ing there for an hour, and came out with throat and lungs unaffected. The apparatus is said to be simple, and not• likely to get out of order. - . CAUSE OF SENILITY IN MAN.

MM. Henry and Bastien have been investi- gating the cause of senility in man, and con- tend that it is due to dehydration of the tissues, and consequent mineralisation. 1 And this de- hydration, or loss of water, is brought about by the diminishing Activity of certain ferments, whose work it is to fix water chemically on the nutriment, and thus render it assimilable. When their activity diminishes a part ,of the water now held in the tissues gy capillary action only tends to evaporate, and hence the dehydra- tion. A further result of the growing inactivity of the ferments is loss of weight. The cause of senility being thus known, the co-operation, of chemist and biolotrist should be ago to suggest a remedy. It is to be noted, however, com- ments the Globe, that science has hitherto shewn itself more skilful in finding out the cause of the various ills of life than in provid- ing cures.

A PRINCELY DEVOTEE OF SCIENCE. .-The Prince of Monaco is a devotee of science, and cruising over the ocean in his yacht, he has gleaned no small harvest of scientific knowledge from the sea. Following some sperm whale fishers on one occasion, he had the good fortune to be present at the capture. He secured por- tions of the last meal of the monster, which turned out to be fragments •or ',gigantic and hitherto unknown cuttlefish. His Serene High- ness publishes , regularly in the " Comptes Rendus " of the Frenc}l Academy a summary of his work in the Atlantic • and Mediterranean. A record of eighty-two soundings and nineteen sets of temperature observations sliew that the time has not been spent idly. Samples of air and water have also been collected and ex- amined. One of the most interesting results, perhaps, is the discovery of a new submarine bank near the Azores; which may some day be- come a valuable fishing-ground. More recently the Prince's yacht has hen instrumental in dis- covering some deep - serc•Cephaloriods with very remarkable luminous organs.

TIIE NATURE • OF ELECTRICITY.

If electric phenomena are •different from gravi- tativo or thermal Or luminous phenomena it does not follow, says the Scientific American, that electricity is miraculous or that it is a sub- stance. We know pretty thoroughly what to expect from it, for it. is as quantitatively related to mechanical and thermal and luminous pheno- mena as they are to cacti other; so if they arc conditions of matter, the presumption would be strongly in favour of electricity being a condi- tion cr property of matter, and the question. "What is electricity?" would then be answered in a way by saying so, but such an answer would not he the answer apparently expected to the question. To say it was a property of matter would be not much more intelligible than to say the same of gravitation. At best, it would add another property to the list of properties we al- ready credit it with, as elasticity. attraction, and so on. In any case, the nature of electricity re- mains to be discovered and stated in terms com- mon to other forms of phenomena, and it is to be hoped that lonz before this new century shall have been completed, mankind will be able to form as adequate an idea of electricity as it now haa of heat.

===== 0002601_19051118_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
MYSTERIES OF PHOSPHORESCENCE.

Animal phosphorescence is one of the most surprising and least understood of Nature's phenomena. The pale. bluish-white light of ' the glowworm has been found by naturalists to be dependent upon some iy,rvous action, regulated at pleasure by the • inseat,'" for it has the power of obscuring it entirely.. If the glowworm 1)0 crushed and the face' or h^.ncls rubbed with it, luminous streaks like those produced' by phos- phorus will appear. They sh:ne more brightly in oxygen gas and in nitrous acid. The power of emitting luminous rays belongs to several varieties of fly and three species of beetle of, the genus Elater. One of the most brilliant of these is the great lantern fly of South America, which gives enough light to enable a person to read by the rays from a single insect. Numerous classes of these . flies are found in Surinam, where they illuminate the darkness in a most remarkable manner. In some of the bogs of Ireland, says Health, lives a worm which gives out a bright green light. There are mans other kinds of . Insects. which become luminous in the dark. Yet there are many curious instances of phosphorescence in dead animal and vegetable matter, the lobster among crustaceans, the whiting among fishes, and decayed wood being striking examples. Their emission of light seems to depend, however, not upon putrefac- tion, for as this progresses their luminosity diminishes, but upon certain unknown a-tino- spheric conditions.,

===== 0002601_19051118_art0020.txt | included=true | included =====
WOMEN'S CHAT.

The Queen and Her Family.—There will be a large party at Sandringham for the Queen's birthday on December Ist, when Her Majesty will have as many as possible of her children and grandchildren with her, The two eldest grandchildren—the Ladies Alexandra and Maud DUI!, who will now bear the rank of Princesses, are growing big girls, and Lady Alexandra is now in her fifteenth year. She is not dynastically so important a young person as she was before the marriage of the Prince of Wales, but she is heiress— presumptive to her father's dukedom, and his earldom of Macduff. Though the King has conferred the title of Princess Royal upon his eldest daughter, it is under- stood that the Duchess of Fife will continue, as far as possible, to merge her Royal rink in that of a British peeress. Like her sister Princess Victoria, she seldom appears at Court functions, and is a home-loving Princess de7oted to her family, and quiet country life. King George and his Children.—The King of the Hellenes has never: spent much time in this couutry but we have had several visits from members of his family. and, on the present occasion his Majesty is accom- panied by his son and daughter-in-law, Prince and Princess Nicholas of Greece. Not long since the Crown Prince and Princess were over here. They were married in 1889. The Crown Princess is a sister of the German Emperor, and has a family of three sons, and two daughters,so that, like his royal sister Queen Alexandra the King of Greece is a grandparent of many years standing. Prince Nicholas is his third son, who married the only daughter of the Grand Duke Vladitnir, and his wife is therefore first cousin to the Czar of Russia, King George's second son is married to the eldest daughter of Prince and Princess Louis of Battenburg. The King of Greece has thus followed the example of his father, the King of Denmark, in marrying his children into the leading retgning families.- Exile Scarcely a Misfortune. —Princess Nicholas of Greece is a sister of the Grand Dike Cyril of Russia, who is in disgrace for marrying Princess Victoria Melita of Coburg, the divorced wife of the Grand Duke of Hesse. It is however scarcely a misfortune to be an I exile from Russia at the present moment, where the existing state of things must be amuse of grave anxiety for all who have friends or relatives there. The Grand Duke Cyril is the eldest son of the powerful Grand Duke Vladimir, and it is no doubt due to the influence' of the Czarina that he is an exile under the hen of the Czar's displeasure. The Grand Duke of Hesse is the Czarina's brother, but Princess Melita's marriage with him was an unfortunate one for her. It is now under- stood that the Grand Duke Cyril is negotiating for the purchase of an estate near Coburg, and another in Bavaria, where he and Priricess Melita will spend most of their time in.future._

-.-- The Old-Fashioned Fairy Tales.—The Duchess of Somerset is quite at variance with the Duchess of Buckingham on the subject of ghost stories and fairy tales as suitable reading for young people. Such read- ing she considers is only filling children's minds with nonsense, but the Duchess of Buckingham has quite recently published a volume of stories which gave her delight in her childhood, and which she believes will be equally entertaining for the children of the present day. i There s, however, no doubt that education, and the prevailing scepticism of the age are responsible for the gradual decadence of this class of literature for chil- dren. There are still young people in the world, hut they ask more for facts, and seem less incliaed to believe in the fairy tales which at least had the merit of laying up a store of pleasant memories for those whom they amused in a less prosaic age. Notable Sportswomen.—The Duchess of Somerset is a Scotswoman, and married the Duke, then Mr. Algernon St. Maur, in 1577, some seventeen years before he succeeded to the title. Her great delight in those early years, was to accompany her husband on his big- game shooting expeditions, often " roughing it," and on one occasion she herself saved a critical situation by a well-planted shot. The Duchess of Newcastle has elso been on similar expeditions, and another notable sports- woman is the Duchess of Bedford. Lady Hindleys and Lady Delarnere both went in for sporting honeymoons, spending theirs in Africa, where they brought down some fine trophies, and this season Lady Waterford is going with her husband on a big-game shooting expedi- tion in Somaliland. Lady Waterford is the youngest daughter of Lord and Lady Lansdowne, and married Lord Waterford some eight years ago. Bridesmaids, and Maids of llonour.—Grelt diversity of opinion reigns among the brides of to-day, with regard to bridesmaids, some arguing that in "splendid isolation" the bride appears to far greater advantage, while others assert that a train of "maids" has a more. striking effect. One recent bride, holding the latter view, not only indulged in a train of twelve "maids," but added to the procession, four matrons of honour. Two were sisters of the bride, the other two being sisters of the bridegroom, the four pretty young women wearing rose coloured chiffon-velours gowns, their coiffured heads, being crowned with most becoming lace caps, and their hands tucked into enormous granny muffs built of lace to match that employed for the caps. With regard to the trousseau, many prospective brides are availing themselves of a new idea, which is finding favour with some leading London shops, viz., a complete outfit, at a certain price. One or two establishments are making a speciality of a £5O trousseau and unquestionably if this comprehensive outfit was purchased little by little and here and there, much less would be obtained for the money. A charming Accessory.—A delightful substitute for the costly feather boa is the crocheted specimen, which, for *welling wear looks quite as well,and is most beauti- fully warm. A very thin, and slightly rough, yet withal soft wool is used, and tho work is easy to accomplish— Needless to say these boas look their best in white, cream, or very pale colours, and they should he very full, well shaped, and long At the moment these new boas are being shown in many leading and most exclu- sive London shops, and high Napoleonic collars, lined with the same description of work are being added to some of the latest evening capes and coats. Directions how to proceed may be found in several of the periodicals devoted to crochet and knitting. About Blouses.—Vast numbers of blouses built of soft Japanese silk and Valenciennes lace, are being offered in the shops just now, at remarkably low prices, the reason for this procedure lying in the fact that the

ves are hopelessly out of date. The woman or girl :ho has to dress inexpensively would however do well to buy some of these dowdy looking garments, taking care that the yoke portion is of a shape most becoming to them. The first, thing to do, is to match the material, an easy task generally, and take out the sleeves. Next, a pair of close, well-fitting lining sleeves—to the elbow only—are required. The portion from wrist to elbow, usually left semi-transparent to match tilt yoke, can be constructed out of the old sleeves, therefore a couple of yards of silk is ample to provide the modish full puff, from shoulder to elbow, and the frills that finish it off there. Every woman should provide herself witha few well-fitting and well-boned silk slips, and when the blouse is drawn down upon one of these, and carefully tacked round the waist to prevent slipping and bagging, this up-to-date garment should leave little to be desired. _ _

Beautiful Fabrics.—The Wylwyrwell Frieze Serge, and Wylwyrwell Habit Cloth, we heard so much about in the Emily autumn are enjoying a great vogue, and deservedly, for these fabrics, which neither spot with rain, shrink or fade, make up into the most effective and charming costumes. Both fabrics are fifty inches in width, and a wide range of colours, all fast dye, is available, both in the cloth and serge. Messrs. Lutas Leathley and Co.'s woollen crepe, too,—another speci- ality—will come as a boon and a blessing to the home dressmaker. The material, 28n. wide and only Is. Id. a yard, mskes up into the most charming evening gowns and blouses, the long range of colours being very soft and beautiful. Upon receipt of a postcard sent to Armley, Leeds, this well-known firm will for- ward a box of patterns vrhich need not be returned. MAD GE.

STABBING AFFRAY AT HELSBY.

A Village Sensation.

Od Saturday night a serious stabbing affray startled the usually quiet village of Efelsby, as a result of which W. Hyde, employed as a mechanic at the works of the British Insulated and He'shy Cables, Ltd., now lies in a serious condition at the Chester Infirmary. It appears that about ten o'clock in the evening Hyde left the club and institute in company with an employee at the same works, and they proceeded together to their homes, which adjoin one another in Crosland-terrace, close by. On reaching their houses some quarrel appears to have taken place, and,although their wives, attracted by the noise, came out and endeavoured to separate them, a pocket-knife was drawn and Hyde was stubbed in the stomach. Dr. Briant, who was at once sum- moned, found the injuries of such a serious nature that he ordered Hyde's removal to the Chester Infirmary, whence he was conveyed in the Cable Company's motor- car, and now lies in a critical condition. His assailant was afterwards arrested and taken to Frodsham Police Station. The affair has caused a great sensation throughout the village.

===== 0002601_19051118_art0024.txt | included=true | included =====
Moral Instruction in Cheshire Schools. •

The Cheshire Education Committee, which mat on Monday at Crewe, after considerable discussion, decided to withdraw from the schools generally the books of moral lessons for children by Mr. F. J. Gould, making it optional on managers to use them if they so wished. It was agreed on a vote to adopt a recommendation/ of the Higher Education Committee: —" That for the purpose of an erection, extension, and equipment of secondary schools the charges should be borne out of the higher education fund, in the same way as the maintenance of such schools, the country being treated as a unit."

WEEKLY REPORT ON THE HEALTH OF WARRINGTON.

WEEK ENDING NOVEMBER 11th 1905. During the week, the following births and deaths •were registered :—Warrington Registration District births, 29, deaths, 28. The excess of births over deaths was I. The birth-rate was equal to 218. The death-rate from all causes was equal to 21.0 per 1,000 against 14.8 the average of the pre v ieus 6 weeks and against 31'7 the corresponding period of last year. The death rate from the seven chief zymotic diseases was equal to 2•2 per 1,000, against 28 the average rate of the previous 6 weeks and against IPG 'tine corresponding period of 1901. The ages of mortality were:- 0-1 1-5 5-15 15-25 25-65 65—up. Total 6 3 2 11 6 28. The deaths included-1 from whooping consh, 1 from enteric fever, 1 from diarrbms, 3 from phthisis, 1 from other tubercular diseases, 2 from cancer, 1 from lung diseases. 1 from accident, 17 from all other causes. The following cases of infectious diseases have been .reported during the week :—Scarlet fever 8 cases, diphtheria 1 case, phthisis 1 case. Total number of patients in hospital, week ending 11th November, 1905, 41; Warrington 21: Newton, 18 ; 'Rural District, 2. METEOROLOOY.—The mean temperature was 44 Fah. :and therefore 1 deg. lower than the preceding week. The maxim= temperature 52 F., was recorded on the v7th inst.., and the minimum temperature 36 F., ou the Ilth inst. The rainfall measured PO5 inch. The general direction was S.W.

J. GITEST GORNALL, Medical 021cer of Health.

CHAPTER XXV.—Conlinus

"No one much this time," said a man nee. her in a relieved voice, and his words seemed to rasp every nerve in her body. " What's wrong, my dear ? Bina. gasped, and the crowd seemed to go round before her dizzy brain. Then with a tremendous effort she mastered herself, warding off an inclination to faint. _ _ _

" My fiance," she managed to say through her dry lips in a hard unnatural voice to the man who had asked her what was wrong. " I-Tusl,, not a word. Oct me a hansom."

"At his rooms in Half Moon Street," came the reply.

He handed Bina in t.nderly, hncl Mike jumped in after her. .

" do my Ltist, mis 3," replied the ser- , grant sympathetically, on the alert in an instant. " Poor girl, poor girl." he muttered as he took the horse's head; "she's got a rough time in front of her. .

By means of a stentorian voice and the aid of a mounted comrade a passage was soon made through the crowd, Which gave way with ready sympathy as soon as they ' realised whither the girl in the cab was bound. . . _ . .

"Why itis‘ Lady Bina Belsize," said one man, recognising her well-known features; and in a flash it was all over the crowd. "

Bina • jumped out at the- door and rang the bell softly. It Was opened by an elderly man, who was obviously an old soldier. " I am Lady. Sabina Belsize," she \\said simply, stepping inside to the man's astonish- ment. "I am engaged, to Lord Ben Nevis. I have come to nurse him." And within an half an hour she was installed in the sick -room beside the unconscious man, and a messenger was on his way to fetch Lady Grizelle and her maid, and to announce her

whereabouts to the Duke and Duchess of Hampstead. Then began the fight with death.

announced the intention of the Government to devote the evening sitting to the subject. The news set everyone talking, and the whole of London was agog with excitement. Hardly ever, even at the time of the Home Rule debate, had. the House been so crowded or so stuffy as it was that evening at nine o'clock ;iuicl every member was in his seat. The peers' gallery was croWded, all the members of the cabinet in the Upper House being present, including -Lord Struan and Lord Stepney. The Duke of !Hampstead, pale and excited, was seated immediately over the clock ; and Lord Swanage as in the background. In the Distinguished Strangers' Gallery Sit Hugo Verschoyle, with ills strong, stern face, was conspicuous ; and only to seats from the roan, who was practically being impeached, at Otto, outwardly calm and collected, with lan Frazer by his side. RUGBY UNION.

FRIENDLY MATCH.

STOCKTON HEATH V. SALE "A."—The Stockton Heath club journeyed to Sale, on Saturday, and after a fast and exciting game were beaten by six point to three. Sale kicked off, and were soon on the attack. Good kicking by the hacks relieved the pressure, and Stockton Heath took play to the home lines. Sale soon returned to the attack, ad from a line-out the wing man secured the hall and scored under the posts after running half the length of the field. The goal kick failed. Half- time : Sale "A," three points ; Stockton Heath, nil. After the restart, Stockton Heath attacked. but found the defence too strong. Sale, by good forward work, took play to half-way, where the hacks secured and breaking through. scored under the posts, the kick again Stockon Heath now pressed, and from scrambl- ing play S. Haelehurst scored, H. Davenport had hard lines with the goal kick, the ball only going inches out. Stockton Heath were now playing a fine, game, but although continually on the attack failed to break through, and time was called with the score : Sale .` A," six points. Stockton Heath, three points.

OUR LOCAL CHURCHES.

St. John's Sunday S:hool Annlvanary Services.

On Sunday the Sunday school anniversay services were held at St. John's. The Rev. J. La Milne, preached in the forenoon :tad addressed the Sunday school scholars in the afternoon. in the evening the preacher was the Rev. J. J. Glover, the recently inducted minister of the historically interesting Presby- terian Church at Risley, near Warrington. Speaking from the text" For we are labourers together with God," Mr. Glover said there was no gettin., on in life without work. To every man his work—that was God's great Libour law. If a man did not work, and work hard, he was soon thrust aside and fell into the rear. No one who kept his eyes open could fail to see that their cleverest wen and women were men and women of great industry and unflinching perseverance. All true men and women were called of God to do their work in the world, and whatever their work was they should seek to please God,and to honour If irn in the doing of it. They were God's fellow-labourers. God gave to each and all of them their work and he expoc.ted them to put their hearts into it, and to give to him of their very best. That was so in strictly religious work. Every num and every woman who made a profession of Christianity bore a great responsibility. They were put in trust with the gospel of Jesus Christ. What were they doing with that gospel ? It was for others as well as for themselves, and by telling others—by giving it to others, they were making Weir own possession of it all the more sure. They were God's fellow-workers, and let. it not be .forgotten that Paul spoke not to church officers. but to all church members. Officers were appointed to do things which could only be done through representatives ; all the church work did not belong to them, and the nearer a church came to having no need of officers the more nearly did that church realise the divine ideal. All had their work to do in the church. The church wanted men and women who would enter heart and soul into Sunday school work, men an I women who would shepherd with tender care those little lambs of the flock.—The church wanted men end women who would go after absent people and absent scholars, and carry the gospel to those who would not enter the church doors—the church wanted angels of mercy to go to homes of sickness and sorrow and dis- tress. Might the inspiration for Christian work and Christian service be theirs in that church, for Christ's sake and for the good of His cause. At the evening service the choir sang the anthem "I will extol Thee." Mr. J. W. Jewett presided at the organ. Satisfactory collections were obtained for Church and Sunday school funds.—

A case of suicide occurred at New Brighton on Wed. nesday, a young German, named Hans Mantimor, being found dead in an hotel bedroom, shot through the head with A revolver.

At a meeting of the Council of the Wirral and Birken- head Agricultural Society held on Tuesday it wee resolved to hold the Society's next show at Bebington on Wednesday the 18th and Thursday the 19th of July, 190 G.

ROADS AND ROAD LOCOMOTION.

Motorists Criticised.

An interesting lecture on "Roads and Road Locomo- tion " WAS delivered before the members of the Literary and Philodbphical Society on Nionclay eveaing by Dr. W. ft. Ormandy, late Bishop Berkeley Fellow of Owens College. The President (Alderman Bennett) presided, and at the commencement of the meeting ruade sympa- thetic references to the late Mr. Pater Stubs and the late Mr. Thomas Birchen, who were members of the Society. Dr. Orrat ANDY pointed out that during the last 00 years the great arteries for traffic in this cuuntry which had been started were practically ail. The roads were in a better state at the time of the introduction of railways than at the preseet time. Contrasting our system of road supervision with that in force in France, he poiuted out that in France a Minister of Roads and Bridges WAS appointed and he divided the work into six classes—national main roads, provincial main roads and four minor classes. The national main roads were built at the national expense while the provincial roads were also made at the expense of the nation but ve3re engineered and looked after to the greater part by the provinces. They were,all, however under the coutrol of the head authorities. The other roads were looked after by the provincial authorities but were entirely paid for by or had large grants from the Government be aid in their up-keep. In England we had about two-thirds the amount of main road they had in France and in the so-called up-keep of it we spent about one and a third times as much as was needed to keep the wonderful roads of France in ardor. The great reason was that a vast amount of money was frittered away in this country in establishment charges which were bowed to be produced when we had such tot enormous number of authorities. There were 1835 road authorities in England and Vales, exclusive of greater London,and the great north road between London and York was con- trolled by 72 different.authorities. The method of managing our roads was not one which deserved , any need of praise. The questions of congestion in industrial centres and the housing of the working classes depended to a large extent on the finding of good roads and rapid means of progression along them. In many districts entirely new roads were required to reduce the oongestion on the existing rounds. Whether the commission now sitting would advocate that there should be some central authority, that many of the• local authorities should have their power reduced, and even those which had a certain amount of power left should be compelled to work under the instructions of some central authority capable of commanding sufficient knowledge to ensure that roads should not only be , built-on the best lines, bat kept up on the best lines, he did not knew. The amount spent on the up-keep of the roads was 12 millions ayear and they ought to beable to do I a little bit of road-scraping for that. ( Laughter). Modern progress, continued Dr. Ornaandy,e had shown that there were methods of banding roads which removed the dust nuisance but the one diiliculy wres the prime cost of the improved method. The hatter road, however, was cheaper in the long run. Mail the roads were managed and maintained by a central authority it was hopeless to expect that such roads would be made and so long as complaints were so rife about municipal extravagance there was not much chance of getting the roads A number of lantern views illustrative of the development of road locomotion were exhi bited.showing that many years ago forms of motor traffic were in vogue in this country, but they fell almost into disuse owing to the preindice against them. Mr. THOnAS MAY, in proposing a vote of thanks to the doctor for his able and interesting paper, said the question of the roads was a mighty one, and the sooner we placed them on a proper footing and maintained them in the most scientific and inexpensive manner, the better it would be for the progress of this country. (Hear, beer.) Mr. J. Moony,: MURRAY seconded.

I Mr. C. WHITE supported and said the roads had not been very good here for a long time as in 1750 a traveller wrote that he could not drive from Warrington to Winwiec without getting up to the axles in mud. , Mr. Rvmert. YOUNG said he had no prejudice against I the motor, but he was not prepared to say that the world was to sink all its obligations, rights and privi- leges because a few men liked to scamper all over the country. (Hear, hear, and laughter.) If regulations could be made to enable the motorists to travel with I safety to the public, he saw no reason why they should I not go along at any rate possible, but they must remem- ber that railways, some of which did not exceed 30 or 40 miles an hour, had to be fencod off. They Mite told that it was childish to object to motorists travelling along the country roads at satin greater speed, but it was their duty to think of the people, the children, and even the dogs. (Laughter and applause.) The Rev. C. HARVEY COOK said that in view of the fact that railways were confined to making roads for themselves he failed to see the justice which would admit of the English road being used at tremendous paces by motor cars. If motorists 4ished to travel at that pace they ought to bear the same exneuse as railway com- panies and provide their own roads. The CLIAIRNIAN said that if they could dispense with the unpleasant features of motoring—such as the dis- figuring of the occupants of the cars, the dust nuisance, and the offensive smell—their objections would be cozy much modified. With a spirit of " give and take "they ought to be able to arrive at suitable regulations. He however; thought aerial locomotion would be the ultimate solution of the difficulty. VDr. ORAIANDY, in replying to the criticisms, said he had little doubt that in the immediate future great pro- gress would be made in the direction of dust prevention. If the p)1 ice would summon impartially all people who drove to the dancer of the public they would have no keener supporter than the average motorist.. After all. the average motorist was not a " road hog " and motorists as a body had to suffer more than anyone else for the sins of the few. Excessive speed was largely due to the fact that we had educate I no motor drives in this country and we had to get foreign drivers who were used to the wild speeds possible oa their own good roads.

Band of Hope Meeting.

The first entertainment in connectioa with the Legh-street Primitive Methodist Band of Hope Society was held on Tuesday evening. Mr. Thomas Dale pre- sided. Mr. Walter Dale said he hoped the young people of the church would stand up for the Band of Hope. Miss Lloyd Williams's orchestra gave several selections, which were very much appreciated. The soloists wcre the Misses Lavinia Hewitt, Madge Davies and Ada Titchett. The Misses Elsie and Hilda Spruce and Miss May Isherwood and Master A. Whitfield sang duets, while violin solos were given lit- Mr., William Delooze and Arthur Pickering. Master harry 'Tongs gave a pianoforte solo, and the children of the , Band of Hops were heard to advantage in a number of recitals. The Stockton Heath Band of Hope Society gave a humorous sketch, entitled " Jolty Factory Lasses," and a dialogue was given by mem- bers of I.igh-street Band of Hope, entitled " The Midnight Train."

Mr. BA tFoux's speech A CONTEMPTIBLE at Newcastle will excite

GAME. no feeling of surprise. There may be, and there ought to bA, some disappointment, even among honest Conservative politicians. The Prime Minister began by suggesting that we are face to face with a General Election, which means that he

has again; taken his orders from Mr. CHAMBER'. LAIN. He knows, of course, when the General Election will take plac4. The time is at his own disposal, under the orders of his master; hut he is wholly incapable of making a candid statement. He will not be frank with his own followers. It is due to his cousin, Lwd [luau CECIL and all those who believe that Balfourisru is vitally ddfareot from Chamberlainism—it is still more due to thei country, of course—that we should be told no only when the General Election is to be fought but upon what issue. There are those who have been sanguine enough to suppose that Mr.BALFous , would have made a cleau breast of it at Newcastle.' He did nothing of the kind. He made the speech' of a political partisan. He said insulting and foolish things about his opponents, but he fenced all the time with the questions on which he was. expected, and on which it was his duty, to make, definite declarations. Yet we must do him the justice to say tha4; if any meaning whatever can be attached to his words, he has deprived the Free Fooders of the slightest excuse for supporting what they call a Balfourian policy. Calling upon his party to sink their differences, and putting into choicer ; language the Chief Whip's advice about "fighting with tails up," he advises his followers to take no : part in fiscal debates or in discussions of anY question not affecting the present state of affairs.

What does that mean? That the Prime Minister

desires to withhold from the country the question which Mr. CHAMBERLAIN is resoluta OH putting to it? That if he has surrendered as to the General

Election, he intends to hold his own on the fiscal

I issue, and to keep it out of the progratnme to be set before the people when the apred to the country actually takes place? Not at all. lie i preaches Retaliation. Ile denouncscl the doctrine i of laissezfaire which he says is ar.tiquated ana illusory. Finally, he says that the New Uoionist , Party, using the phrase for the first time, should . be free to deal with the restrictions which hamper', industry and should follow out a policy which if; calculated to draw the Empire more eloeelY togethtn. This is Mr. CITAMBERLAIN'S programme ) in full, but dressed up in a speech wh:ch has Mr. CHAALBEILLAIK'S frankness.

There can now be no excuse for the continUed and dog-like faithfulness of those Conservative members of Parliament whose belief in All'

BALFOUR'S OICVOTOOSS led them to conceive that ha

was leading the member for West Birmingbani into a trap. When Mr. CHAMBERLAIN declared that his views and those of the Prime Minista were substantially the same, he knew what he WO saying. He was obviously speaking by Pre-) arrangement. He knew what Mr. BALrouu hog

eel sedulously keeping from his friends. No more contemptible game has ever been played by a Prime Minister than that which may be con- sidered to have cone to an end in the triumph of the author of the raging and tearing cam- paign. Mr. BALFOUR says that he is not a Protectionist. But that is not how Mr. CHAM- ISERLAIN describes himself or Mr. CHAPLIN, or the members of the Tariff Reform League. "Protec- tionist' is a name that is shrunk from on all hands. It raises too many cruel memories ! it recalls too much public suffering. But Protection is the word which fits Mr. BALFOUR'S policy as precisely RS it fits that of Mr. CHAMBERLAIN. The Prime Minister shivers at the word; the nation shivers at the policy. Fortunately there is no doubt among even the Government aud its followers that it will be shattered et the polls, under what- ever name its preseut exponents may endeavour to disguise it. With his characteristic self-approval the Prime Minister told his hearers at Newcastle that if the Conservative Party does not follow his advice, it'will be overtaken by disaster. But as was evident from some passages of his speech, he is thoroughly well aware that it will be overtaken by disaster in any case. And, in the opinion of the country, the sooner the ',better.

While the Govern- ROYALTY AND THE ment courteously UNEMPLOYED. acknowledges the gravity of the -unemployed problem and expresses sympathy with the sufferers, and Labour M.P.'s rack their brains to discover work-producing schemes which are not too fantastic for practical politics, the workless and the hungry gloomily, almost desperately, contemplate the rigours of an approaching -miter. But the star of hope has dawned and is mpidly approaching a zenith which promises well for all immediate necessities. Her Most 'Gracious Majesty the Queen has exercised the divine prerogative of Royalty, and herself inaugur- ated a relief fund with the magnificent donation •of £2,000 while the King and the Prince of Wales have followed suit with sums of £2,000 and 4000 respectively. The royal example is indicative— where an indication is not necessary—of Her Majesty's real appreciation of the needs of the poorest of her people,and it has met with a national response which bids fair to make the fund equal to coping with all the most urgent metropolitan and provincial necessities. Already something like £30,000 has been subscribed, and the money is rolling in at the rate uf nearly £lO,OOO a day. So much for a noble example in a truly Christian cause!

At ter Wednesday's STOCKTON HEATH enthusiastic meeting LIBERALISM. at Stockton Heath

there can be no doubt that Liberalism is making great headway •in what has hitherto been regarded as a Tory stronghold—the Knutsford Division. The meeting was certainly the best which the party has held in the locality for some years, and should inspire the workers in this part of the large and scattered ,constituency to even greater efforts to secure the return of Mr. A. J. King when the trial of strength comes. The speaking was excellent and made a great impression —even upon many with Protec- tionist leaoings who attended the meeting. In Tree Trade Liberals have a great cause to defend, for it is upon that policy that the commercial prormerity of the country is based. The adoption of Retaliatory measures, it was clearly shown, would be disastrous to British trade and a specific instance of "hat the damaging effect would be upon at least one important local industry was given. Mr. A. J. King, prospective candidate, delivered a well•rearioned address, proving that he is a close 'student of economics and the great questions of the day, and we are confident that in him the 'Knntsford Division would have a capable and conscientious representative. We are pleased to welcome the appearance in the political arena of 'r. W. Madden—a Radical of Radicals as he des- ertred himself. A powerful and convincing :speaker, he will prove a valuable asset of the party in this district, and we hope that in a short time the Liberals of Warrington will have the benefit .of his eloquent advocacy of the claims of Liberalism on behalf of their own caodidate.

Mr. •. .liribed

The local branch of THE FREE TRADE Lianun. the Free Trade League, which pro— vides a common platform for members of all political parties, is actively pursuing its props- ,ganda, undeterred by small audiences. Free Trade and Protection though they so closely affect the everyday life of the individual, do not make a really attractive evening programme. Neverthe- less, the league is steadily, if slowly, helping to cement the people's allegiance to the policy which has dono so much to make Eogland pre-eminent among nations and her people the most comfortable and content on the face of the globe. The Libcrals are doing the same thing on a larger scale but the Doke of -Devonshire's league spreads a finer net and catches many a sprat which its political allies would miss. The chairman of Tuesday's meeting at Brick-street was Alr. Linnams Greening, who aforetirne has been known as a loyal Conservative. We do not doubt that he is one to this day, but he is an honest Free Trader first, and when he declares it the duty of the League to return a Free Trade candidate for Warrington he leaves no room for questioning where his vote will be cast on the day of election. There are many Conserva- tives of 111 r. Greening's way of thinking, hut we doubt whether many of them will put principle before party at the crucial moment as he is pre- -pared to do. Nevertheless there is a very solid body of Free Trade opinion in the town notwith• standing nlausiole arguments advanced by local iron-masters and we believe that when the Free Trader and the hasty whole-hogger—Mr.Pierpoint —are opposed in Warrington the former will gain the day.

U

It it gratifying to BEA.UTIFUL WAREINGTON. hear that the work of the Beautiful Warrington Society is being taken up with con- siderable enthusiasm. Alth-mgh to the outsider it may not appear that the project is much nearer realization, we hear that the committees entrusted With various branches of the scheme have made much progress with the preliminary arrangements, and in the not very distant future we may hope to see Warrington a more attractive if not. an actually " beautiful "town. The townspeople are ,encouragerl to advance the effort by interesting themselves in the cultivation of their gardens and displaying window boxes and baskets of plants and flowers which would do so much to relieve the dullness of some of the streets. It is also hoped to plant trees in the thoroughfares and upon spaces and one committee is actively addressing itself to the problem of smoke abatement. We are particu- larly pleased to learn that the co-operation of the .school children is being invited by the Society. Too often the efforts of those who try to beautify their surroundings are marred by the destructive- ness of children, and the Society has taken a Commendable step in forming a children's auxiliary, the members of which will be under a promise not to damage trees and plants. chalk on tbe walls, or perpetrate any similar misdeeds.

OUR ROADS.

Like "our boys our roads constantly • require attention and are by no means all that they should be. Dr. Ormandy, in the course of a lecture before the Literary and Philosophical Society, had some interesting and instructive things to say about roads and road locomotion. We agree with him when he says that roads are not as good as they were a hundred years ago, though like him we d not positively recollect the state of the road-surface 80 much anterior to the advent of the steam- 'engine and the motor-car. The roads were good then and they are bad now—that suffices for the argument if the roads do not suffice for the traffic. Not only has the science of road-making

failed to make much headway in the last Lucky Lancaster! Mr. Thomas Barrow, a memller hundred years. but we h Ave not yet of the Society of Friends, has generously offered to learnt the wisdom of oentralising the control give a site for a Y.M.C.A. and a sum of £l,OOO of the great arteries for locomotion. For instance towards the cost of the new building. It would be a the great north road from London to York graceful act on the part of some wealthy Warring. is controlled by 72 different authorities who all have tonian to'follow the good elample of the gentleman their own ideas of road-making and exploit them named. Neff said! accordingly. This is had for the north road. Then there are such things as stupid wooden swing bridges over small canals—such as the one at Sankey Bridges—which fill the part of the weak link in the chain and render a great road quite unfitted for the traffic which requires to use it. Apparently we have forgotten much that the Romans taught us about roads and learnt very little since which is of practical value. Dr. Ormandy has drawn timely attention to our defici- encies in a little thought of direction and we hope his remarks will attract widespread attention.

* * * Mr. W. E. Atkin's services to the Musical Society were fittingly recognized on Tuesday evening, when he was the recipient of a handsome present from the members of the Society and other friends. He has thrown himself most assiduously into the work of the secretaryship for fifteen years, and the high terms in which his services were alluded to were no empty compliments. • * * * What an " unconscionable long time in dying " are the industries mentioned by Mr. Chamberlain as vanishing ! The tinplate manufacture is much better than it was a year ago. According to the Beard of Trade memorandum on the state of the skilled labour market in October, the number of mills is higher than in any month during the last ten years. Mr. Joseph was a "little too previous" in his policy.

NOTES AND NOTIONS.

,A. lady defendant at Crewe on Monday was asked if she had a solicitor. She replied, "No ; I have come to tell the truth." Hard on the lawyers !

* * * The " Times " says : " There is, as Mr. Balfour declares, a common constructive policy on which all Unionists can agree. It represents a practical mini- mum on which they must agree if they are to succeed or even to exist, as one party." What is it?. * Alderman Bennett thinks there is a future for the eying machine. We hope it will be an improvement On the past at any rate. So far the flying-machine seems to be nothing more than an elaborated means to an untimely end.

* * In a case at the Borough Police Court this (Friday) , morning when a man was ordered to contribute 10s. per week towards' the maintenance of his five children in. the Workhouse, it was stated that the family would probably cost the ratepayers hundreds of pounds. * * " What is a friar ? A man who fries fish." The question was put this week by a teacher in a local day school and a promising little girl gave the above answer with alacrity. After this who can contend that religious education in the elementary schools is unnecessary? * * The Clerk of the weather was most unkind on San- day and the unusual spectacle of a Mayoral procession in cabs was witnessed at Warrington The fact that cabs were to be provided in case of rain did not appear to be generally known and the attendance was much smaller than usual. . .

*s* The Warrington Junior Liberal League is goian• strongly forward, and the membership has increased very rapidly. The work which the organization has at present in hand is of an educational character, and valuable papers on important topics have been and are to be submitted.

* * A return with reference to the attendance of boys from the public elementary schools in the Borough at the baths has been prepared. In all, 883 boys have attended the baths this season, each on about ten '.oceasions, and as a result, 79 boys have learnt to ' swim, 122 being already able to do so. * * * The War Office—thqt dead horse whom every body flogs without mercy—is credited or debited with the following choice bit of unconscious humocir An army order dealing with 'machine guns, provided for certain Volunteer corps, advises that where possible mules should he employed to draw them. " When a mule is not available, however," it goes on, " any intelligent non-commissioned officer will do instead." Ahem! *s* The Australian cricketers were obliged to leave the mythical " ashes " behind them, but they took a solid .£9OO each back to the laud of the golden fleece. NoW they are succeeded by their cousins from New Zealand, who are sweeping up the " ashes " of football with a vengeance, and gathering up the shekels as well. Poor old England. Woe is she ! * * * Where are the " big-hearted " men with big purses? Warrington is badly badly in need of a Social Institute where the young men of the town can congregate and spend their evenings in a profitable manner. Other towns possess such institutions but Warrington un- fortunately is without. A central site and a broad, minded committee are needed and valuable citizens will be the result. It is a public requirement which would considerably help to form the life and character of the town and give tone to its -townsfolk.

* * * The intimation in the daily press that the public executioner has been fined ;£4 for casting a disdainful glance at the judge of the Supreme Court of Darmstadt leads to much thought. Supposing, for instance, the• Mayors of Boroughs in this country could inflict punishment for a disdainful glance ! What trouble there would be. We can imagine Councillor Petnberton'having to pay heavily in fines after every Warrington Town Council meeting. At present his disdainful glances at the occupant of the " chair " are frequent and free—and they cost him nothing !

- * * * The National Service Leaguers and semi-conscrip- tionists will need to beware for a Gladstone has risen up against them. We learn that an interesting figure for many reasons at the Oxford Union this term is the young heir of Hawarden, Mr. W. G, C, Glacistone,who often takes part in the debates, and whom a local ctitic pronounces " an extremely promising speaker, formidable and to t7te point, with a calm indicating not dulness but suppressed energy." Mr. Gladstone, who is an undergraduate of New College, made an energetic oration the other evening against compulsory national military service. The young speaker denounced this as disguised conscription.

* * * There is a woman at Moore who deserves the Royal :Humane Society's medal for patience (if it has one, and is really humane.) The other day the swing bridge over the Ship Canal tt Moore was closed for repairs and the good soul waited from 10 a.m. to 3p.m. , before setting off for the Stag Bridge at Walton. From a complaint to the Runcorn Rural Council we learn that this sort of thing is not unusual at Moore, but the long-suffering inhabitants are 'tiring of it. NO wonder ! We dare not imagine what would be said at Stockton Heath or Latchford if the swing- bridges there refused to turn for a whole day? *4*

Alderman Bennett has frequently voiced his love of the beautiful, but this week he has struck a newer and much bolder note. He told the members of the " Lit and Phil," on Monday that "it was sad to see beautiful women made hideous, as they were by the introduction of the motor." Mr. Bennett really blames the necessary but unsightly .goggles affected by the motorist. We agree with him. It is a crying shame I that beauteous woman should so disfigure herself • when taking an airing in the automobile. Could not a society be formed to agitate against the practice in the interest of those unpractical but sentimental mortals who would sooner look at the fair motorist than her unfair and dusty motor ? * *

It used to be a bitter 06/not:tint in Warrington that there could be no co-operation between different com- mittees of the Corporation or we should not see first one department and then another tearing up the streets for the various purposes of gas, water and the like, The matters have, mended of late, fortunately, but they were never so aggravating as they are in Loudon. Places like Warrington which have all their services of gas,water,and electricity in the hands of the muni- cipality have no idea to what inconvenience Landon is put by having these services in different bands. Some figures have been got out to show the magnitude of the evil, and from these it appears that in various parts of the Metropolis this streets are taken up so frequently that in every mile of street there are no fewer than from 20 to 139 openings made every year in each Metropolitan borough. *

It was stated at the Chester Diocesan Conference : on Wednesday, where the growing eril of juvenile : smoking was discussed, that there are five million , child smokers M this country. How such a figure is obtained, we •do not know, brit we do not dispute it. : In nine cases out of ten, the ragged street urchin . will smoke all the " fag-ends " he can get, and ! certain kinds of cigarettes are so cheap ! nowadays that we can quite credit the story of a hair- ! dresser who sold 25 packets a day to children between ! seven and twelve years of age. Thera ought to be a • law to prosecute him, .and to deal with the infant • smoker as well. No wonder that out or :12,000 volun- teers who offered to go to the war in South Africa : from Manchester, only 1200 were ultimately accepted. . Palpitation of the heart due to excessive and early ! smoking was the determining factor in the case of most of the rejected. The injury to the life and man- hood of the nation is serious, and some regulation of the vice of juvenile smoking is imperative. I

- - The services at BalJ-street Wesleyan Church on Sunday will be conducted by Prof. J. Hope Moulton, M.A., D. Lit., and Fellow of King's College Cambridge. Wedding at Christ Church. • A interesting wedding was solemnized quietly at Christ Church, Latchford, on Monday, by the Rev. G. C. Briggs. The contracting parties were Mr. Herbert Denham, second son of Mr. Edward James Denham, Ackers-lane, Latchford, and Miss Ida Merea Prince, only daughter of Mr. James Trayloo Prince, Warring- ton. The bride, who was given away by her brother, Mr. James H. Prince, wsre a blue travelling costume and a white grebe hat. Miss F. Watkins was the bridesmaid and Mr. E. Prince acted as best man. The happy couple later in the day left for Blackpool where the honeymoon is being spent. A large number of handsome presents were received.

The first smoking concert for the winter season in connection with the Warrington Cycling Club was held at the Patten Arms on Thursday evening, Mr. D. Hodge presiding over a large attendance. In past years the Club has established a high reputation in this line and and on Thursday evening the programme was such as to equal, if not surpass, the excellent standard which has been created. Mr. T. It. Ford was responsible for a well-played 'cello solo and also acted as accompanist. Vocal quartettes were given by the Excelsior quartette party, humorous sketches by Mr. Clement George, songs by Mr. Norman Evans (tenor), and Messrs. M. Jones and P. Clarke (baritones). Mr. Handley's English concertina quartette party scored a remarkable success, their efforts being most heartily encored. The artistes were cordially thanked at the close for their services. Wycliffe P.S.A. Society.

There was a good meeting and a good attendance on Sunday last. Mr. J. Hawthorn presided, and spoke encouragingly of the work of the P.S.A., and said a member of a P.S.A. had told him that he and several of his brothers never went to a place of worship until they went to a P.S.A. meeting. Now they are regular attendants both at the P.S.A. and the regular services of the sanctuary. Conncillor James Evans was the speaker, and he spoke upon the necessity of the "New Birth." He also referred to the tendency of the times to make the Lord's Day into one of pleasure and I recreation, also the introduction of golf on Sundays, which the speaker very strongly condemned as the thin end of the wedge to destroy the holiness of the day, and service for God and man. Mr. W. H. Payton Mus. Bac., rendered an organ solo in masteily style. The secretary welcomed one new member, total now 550. Mr. J. Owen also took part in the meeting.

PUBLIC BENEFIT.-lINDER TRH CLOCK FOR BEST BOOTS IN LANCASHIRE.—Advt.

Hearts of Oak Benefit Society. A. public meeting of the members of this Society in Warrington was held at the Blue Lion Hotel, M trket Place, on! Thursday evening last week, when the Delegate Of the District (Bro. T. T. Rogers of Widnes) attended and gave an interesting report of the meetings , of the Society held in London during May and August. The questions of excessive sickness, valuation of Society, and the reduced Sick allowances were dealt with at some length. Several other matters of impftrtance\ were touched upon, all of which were ' satisfactorily receiNed and the delegate's action relative thereto was unanimously endorsed. A vote of thanks ,to the delegate for his interesting report was carried unanimously, after which the meeting discussed the possibility of forming a local organisation for the furtherance of the interests of the Society, and mutual help to local members, and a committee of 12 was deputed to make the necessary arrangements and call together the whole of the members in Warrington, Latelif rd and Stockton Heath, so that a definite scheme may be decided upon. Particulars and date of meeting will be advertised in due course, when it is hoped every good wisher will endeavour to be present. A vote of thanks to Bro. T. A. Wilkinson for presiding over the meeting was unanimously passed, and the first meeting of the society held in Warrington for over 10 years, dispersed with the members hoping for another opportunity of meeting with each other in the near future.

H. M. ASIITON'S LIFO Toxic takes away that tired feeling.—(klvt.) The Warrington Society. _ _ _

A. most inte—ibiting lecture 0):1" Sir Richard Burton " was given by Mr. 0. W. LAthouse, last Friday even- ing at the Old Academy. Hr. J. Rymer Young was in the chair and the attendance was very good. The L,_tc.tu ter traced the career of Burton,who was described as " one of the most daring of explorers, a great writer and a most brilliant Oriental scholar." His acquaintance with the East began when he was sent to India. with a commission in the native army. He very quickly acquired the language and dressed as a native. His face stained with henna; he WAS accustomed to travel amonkst the people, and in this way gained intimate knowledge of their views and customs. A few years later, disguised 113 a- Moslem, Ile made his famous pilgt image to Aroma. His next adventure was a• journey to Harar in Somabland,where he wa4 danger- ously wounded. After his recovsry he served in the Crimean war. At the close.•of the war, the Govern- ment supplied him with funds to explore Central Africa. This was the ,pioneer e%pedition to the interior, and resulted in the discovery of lakes Tanganyika and Nyanza. During the last thirty years of his life he was British Coattail in various places, viz. : Fernando Po, Santos, Damascus, and Trieste. Particulars were given of his life at these consulates, and the . unjust and unfriendly action of the Foreign o:fice in their treatment of him was strongly criticised. As an author his activity was amazing. In addition to his bitoks of travels, he wrote On various other subjects mid published many translations of Eastern works, the mast famous one being the " Arabian Nights," for which he received £lO,OOO. A tribute was paid to Lady Burton, who was his devoted comrade for thirty years, and who, after his death, published the, story of his life. A vote of thanks to the lecturer was proposed by Councillor Fulton-Smith, and supperted. by Alderman Bennett, the Rev. Harvey Cook and Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins. •

THE DREDGING OF THE

RIVER.

SHIP CANAL COMPANY'S APPEAL.

In the Chancery Division of the High Conrt of Justice on Monday before 1.114,1 S Justices Vale-hart William:, Stirling and Cozens-Hardy, the hearing wis resnMed of the Ship Canal Company's appeal from an order of Mr. Justice Byrne, and a cross-notice by the plaintiff in the action of Crosaeld v. the Manchester Ship Canal Com- pany. The Court of Appeal had reversed the judgment of the late Mr. Justice liyrne in an action wherein the plaintiffs were the appellants and the respondents were the defendants, and the action had relation to the dredging of the River Nforley. The Court of Appeal had held that the Corporation of Warrington was ex- cluded from accession to the court, and Lord Justices Williams and Stirling were also of opinion that the proceedings on the part of the traders ought to be stayed, bat Lord Justice Cozens-rdardv held that the court had jurisdiction to entertain the action of the plaintiffs. The matter want up to the House of Lords, who held that the right of the Corporation of Warring- ton to sue was excluded, but that the court had juris- diction to entertain the action of the traders, manu- facturers, and othiArs carrying on /..):ISifie3,3 at or near Warrington. The hearing of the appeal was proceeded with on this basis.

The hearing of the appeal and the arguments of Counsel have been continued each day this week, and were again adjourned on Thursday. 111 r. Cripps. ICC., on behalf of the Ship Cma.l Cara. party, dealt at some length with tr. Jaitioe Byrne's judgment. The company had spent £13,000 per year in trying to carry out the conditions laid down by Mr. Justice Byrne, and he asserted that it was both ituormti- cable and impossible to fully carry out these conditions. It was utterly impossible to give at all times eight feet of water when sometimes two or three feet of silt were brought up, which it took months to dredge away. Directing himself to the question of the alleged deterioration of the water of the river Mersey, counsel said that it was an incontrovertible fact that there had bean no deterioration of the water at low spring tides' and that there had been no interference with the old water level. The evidence showed conclusively that after tho Ship Canal Company had put their house in order, there had been no abstraction of water censegnent upon the work carried out by th 3 Ship Canal Company that could have had any appreciable effect on the level of the water of the river Mersey. lie was bound to say that some of the traders had said that the water was lowered, but in answer to : that he put in the official data of the Mersey Dock Board, fie contended that there had been no perma- nent deterioration of the channel ; that the obligation to maintain was not a guarantee to prevent interference with the depth of the water in the channel on the influx of barge quantities of silt. but an obligation to zise .11 reasonable means to remove such obstructions when they arose ; and, further, that in any case the traders had no separate right of action ; and that there was no evidence that the Canal Company, on receiving notice, did not continue to dredge the river to reasonable satis- faction,

Mr. Bousfield, K.C., on behalf of the respondents, contended that it was perfectly possible for the Canal Company to carry out the obligations imposed upon them to secure an Mt. channel if they would only pro- vide sufficient dredging machinery. The Canal Company had been aware all along of the silting up of the river, and they ought to have made provision for dealing with that. It was perfectly easy to provide a normal state of tho channel by adopting certain measures. What had been done by the Canal Company was to give to the respondents a Gft. navigation instead of an fift. navigation. It could not, therefore, be said that the Canal Company had discharged the obligations to provide an Bft. channnel. Mr. Justice Byrne had decided the case in favour of the respondents as to the effective dredging of the channel, end said that if one dredger was not sufficient two must be employed. At this point the hearing was again adjourned.

THE GRAMMAR SCHOOL.

The School Management Committee having con- sidered the proposals mule by the Governors of the Warrington Grammar school for the amendment, of the draft scheme for the government of that school so that the representation of the local authorities upon the governing body might be proportionate to the amount of their financial aid, it was resolved that, in the event of the annual expenditure for the mainten• ance or the school exceeding the income, the committee will make a grant to the governors to cover the amount of the deficit, provided such grant does not, in any year, exceed the sum of £4OO, and that the governors will continue to maintain the existing sixteen free scholarships and grant additional scholarships to be awarded upon conditions to he arranged between this Committee and the Governors so that the number of free scholars each year reaches 2,0 per cent. of the total number of pupils in the school. GRUESOME DISCOVERY AT WARRINGTON.

CHILD'S BODY FOUND BURIED IN LIME.

Alleged Coneealmmt ol Birth.

A sensational discovery was made at Warrington on Monday, when the police visited 63, Grafton-street,and after a brief search found the skeleton of a child buried in qeick-lime under the pantry-floor. In September of this year a man named Alfred Drury drowned himself 'in consequence, it was stated at the inquest which was held, of the neighbours having connected his name with that of his sister-in-law, a single woman, who had the day previous to his death been confined at the Workhouse. The suspicion of the police was aroused by anonymous letters which were forwarded to the , Cilia Constable and the Coroner,and Detective-Sergeant Barlow and Detective Meacock visited 63, Grafton-at. which was formerly occupied by Drury, his wire,Alicia and his sister-in-law,Beatrice Waterworth,aad made the gruesome discovery of the remains of the child. Later in the day Waterworth and Alicia Drury were arrested on suspicion of having caused the death of the child. They were interviewed by the Chief Constable and made certain statements. Waterworth said : It was • on Saturday, the 19th or 20th of October last year, that I gave birth to something which was told me was a baby girl. Alf and Mrs. Drury came upstairs at the titne,anci I said : "What are you doing ?" and he said : 'Nothing' I said : What have you done with it Mr. Drury ?" and he said : "I have buried it in the pantry." He said: " I never heard it cry." Mrs. Drury was not in the room when he said that he had buried it. One day after Detective Meacock inquired after the child, I said to Mts. Drury : " You will look well if you are found out what has been done" and she started to cry. It was Alf Drury who put the child in the pantry." The statement made by Mrs. Drury ran:—" That child was never born alive. I wrapped th 3 baby up and my husband said he had no money to bury it with. He took it and put it in the front bedroom. I left my husband iu downstairs and Beatrice upstairs. T missed the child, and my husband said: "I have taken it out of the house,"l saw lime in a piece of pacer in the back kitchen. I suspected what had happened and I asked him what had happened in the pantry, and he said : "I have done it for the best, it was still-burn." I knew the body of the child was in the pantry a nimth ago. This is my husband's trouble, my hands are clear. On Tuesday the W/II18!1 were brought before the magistrates, Waterworth on a charge dr concealing the birth Of the child.and Drury with abetting her. The Chief Constable, who applied for a remand for a week, said a 'ruest awful state of things must have existed.

_ . Having heard the facts above detailed, and the statements made by the prisoners, the migistrates granted the remand. • THE INQUEST OPENED. Mr. S. Brighouse opened the inquest at the Police Station on Wednesday evening. Mr. Steel appeared on behalf of the prisoners. The Coroner said perhaps it Nvould be recollected that some time ago he held an inquest on the body of Mr. Alfred Drury,who committed suicide by drowning him- self in the canal at Sankey. On that occasion he interro- gated the widow as. to the reason, if any, for the act. He intended to place her depositions before the jury fit the adjourned inquiry,but ns far as his memory served him Mrs. Drury explained that her sister, Beatrice Waterworth, who lived with her, had got into tronble. The neighbours made certain allegations against Mr. Drury, and he was mach uns.t by them. The jury brought in a verdict of "Temporary insanity." Since then the Chief Constable and he (the Coroner) had received - several anonymous letters, in consequence of which the house where the two women formerly lived was visited. Several of the flooring tiles of the pantry were removed and underneath, at a depth of 13 inches, was found buried in quicklime the remains of a child. Dr. S,uith bad made an examination of the remains and the jury would have to find out, if possible, whether the child had a separate existence. If that were proved they would have to decide whether any person was criminally liable for its death. It was quite impossible to finish the inquest that night. The Chief Constable remarked that he intended to place the matter in the hands of the Town Clerk, who might possibly wish to send the depositions to the Public Prosecutor, but he (Mr. Talbot) hardly thought he would.

Datevtive Irsnolck state I that on iqonlay he in cein- nany With Sergeant- Birlaw visited the house, 13, Graf- ton-street, which was occupied by the two prisoners from July, 1901, to October 13, 1905. They were accom- panied by the owner of the house, George Smith, and George lafoxey, a bricksetter. In the centre of the door of the pantry they had several tiles removed, and found the re name of the child buried in quick.- lime. : Later in the day he and Sergeant Barlow visited 10. Russell-street, and saw the two prisoners. Sergeant Barlow said We have came to see you on a serious matter, and anything you say !nay be used in evidence for or against you. We are .zoing to take you to the police station on suspicion of having caused a• child's death in Grafton-street." Neither of the prisoners male a reply and they were taken to the Police Station where they wereseen by the Chief Constable. They made the statements produced. Afterwards they were cautioned and lharged and Water- worth replied "I did not conceal it." Dury said " Yes she was in my house." The inquest was adjourned until net Tuesday after- noon.
