SALE OF WORK. Successful Efforts by Latchford Baptists.

The annual sale or work in connection with Latch- ford Baptist Chionch, was held in the schoolroom Was. lane ou Thursday afternoon. There was a largo attendance at the opening ceremony which was performed by Councillor Brough. _ • .

The Rev. J. F. Alattliews who presided, remarked , that the sale of work had become an annual institution and as it had bec:ome inevitable it had become necessary by reason of the development or their work. Their income was however increasing and this year was in advance of that of any previous years. If they could persuade the ordinary members of the congregation to subscribe, not large sums, but small amounts regularly and systematically sties of work would become fewer and fewer, matt' though they were as a means of raising fonds. (Applause.) Councillor Brough said he defended sales of work. As something which enabled the poorest and humblest to contribute to the Church they ought nor to be despised. Sales of work afforded facilities for those to help a Church who would not otherwise do so. fie hoped the Chnrch would continue to grow not only in numbers but in influence, :tad that they would become a greater and ever increasing power for good in the neighbourhood. tie had pleasure in declaring the sale open. (Applause.) On the motion of Miss Smith, seconded by Mrs. Pearson Councillor Brough WdS heartily thanked for his services and the business for the day commenced. The stalls, which presented a pleasing appearance, being attractively decorated, were in charge of thee following :—Fancy stall : Mesdames Pearson, S.l Albinson, Harrison, and Shakeshaft. "DANDY DICK." Successful Performances by the Dramatic Society.

It is a sincere pleasure to be able to congratulate the members of the Warrington Dramatic Society upon the addition of a conspicuous success to the record of which they have every reason to feel proud. No local entertaining organization has established a better claim to the support of the townspeople by reason of its willing efforts on behalf of deserving charities, and the annual performances certainly rank amongst the most pleasurable of amateur enterprises. There was a large attendance at the Parr Hall on Wednesday evening to witness the opening production of Mr. A. IV.Pinero's farce " Dandy Dick " which went with a. delightful smoothness throughout. One would like to have seen the hall crowded, for not only should. the worthiness of the object—to aid the funds of the Warrington Infirmary and the local corps of the St. John Atnbulance Brigade —awaken an enthusiastic response but the performance itself reached such a high level as to merit a greater share of public patronage. It is gratifying to know that last (Thursday) evening's attendance showed a coosider- able improvement, and both audiences have been keen to appreciate every point and loud in their expres- sions of praise. " Dandy Dick "marks a distinct advance on the plays which the Warrington amateurs have attetnpted in the last year or two. The choice had a special recommendation in the fact that the Society is particularly rich in talent suited to light, comedy. And " Dandy Dick " is as light as anyone could desire—it is irresistibly amusing from the first moment to the fall of the curtain. There is no title fella for "Dandy Dick," is a race- horse—the innocent cause of all the complications which have to be unravelled. The scene of events is the quiet deanery of St. Marvells, where the Very Rev. Augustin Jedd, D D., lives with his two attractive young daughters. The serenity of the home is dis- turbed by the advent of Georgina Tidman, the Dean's widowed sister, who instead of being the "chastened" lonely woman that the occupants of the Deanery expect, torus out to be quite a sporting character, well keowa on the turf, and, in fact, part owner of " Dandy Dick." It so chances that the horse and its joint owner, Sir Tristram Mardoa, Bart., soon afterwards make their appearauce at St. Marvells, where " Dandy Dick is entered for „the race meeting. In a rash moment the good Dean has been led to promise 'l,OOO towards the restoration of a neighbour- ing church spire bat as he does not possess anything like that amount, and numerous bills are press- ing, lie is placed in a fine predicament. Although it goes sorely against the grain, he at length yields to his sister's advice to raise the much-needed fund by " backing " Dandy Dick —or at any rate entrusting a servant with the commission. The night before the races a fire occurs at the inn where' Dandy Dick " is stabled bat tile liors is reseaed just in time. " Georg? Tid" then iusists upon its being accomina- dr.te'l in the Deanery stable, despite the pro- tests of the Dean against the impropriety of such a coarse. Toe play, of course, is not without its little love affairs which, of course, have their poetical ending. ! The cast is as follows : The Very - Rev. Angustiu D.D. (Dean of SG Ntarvells), Nir. Most of the performers have wan laurels in previous matures, and their reappearance has been heartily welcomed. Mr. Sidney %Valllread scores a splendid success as the Dean of St. Xlarvells indeed, the part. could not have been better placed. His dignity rises superior to all misfortunes—even when he finds himself in the hands of the police—and he invests conversa- tional conatu mplaeos with a lofty impressiveness which is such an essential f6atrire of the. part. He is par- ticularly go )(I in tile scene when, after many misgiv- ings, he commissions Blare to back ” Dandy Dick " and afterwards when, as he sets to work in nig shirt sleeves to mix the " simple remedy," pleasant memories of the old college days are recalled. Miss Kite Brnedrett excels herself as Georgians Tidunn, the Dean's masterful aunt of racecourse fame who( loses no time ie introducing her " tone " in the eminently respectable atmosphere of the deanery. She acts with delightful freedom, and displays fine elocutionary powers, every word being distinctly heard by the audience. She has fully realized the possibilities of the part and has elicited the warmest praise each evening. The Misses Maggie and Bessie Pierpoint maintain the high repatations which they have achieved in the amateur dramatic realm, ful- ' filling the roles of Salome and Sheba, the Deau's daughteits,with charming naturalness. Their ineffectual efforts to coax the Dean to settle their little hills create no little merriment, and there is another • pleasing scene when they welcome their father, on his return h me, with every demon- stration of delight, only to ha taken t ) task a few minutes later for their " deception " in going to the fancy dress ball. Mr. Geoffrey Dodd is really admirable as Sir Tristram Mardon, Bart., the cordial, good-humoured partner of " George Tid " and he hag gained a full measure of the audiences' approval. The dapper Major Tenter, who has frequent convenient. attacks of a malady contracted in India is portrayed by Mr. Victor Roberti who acts exceedingly wed, especially considering that this is his first appearance in part of any importance. Mr. Harold Joseph is also a suc- cess in the role of Mr. Darbey, a typical young officer. Mr. Harry Mosley is always a popular figure in the Society's productidtts and be makes the most of the a part of Blorc, the butler, with a fatal facility for aspirating in the wrong places. He is thoroughly at home on the stage and adds quite a dramatic touch to the play in the midnight scene when ho plisoas the dean's carefully-compounded remedy. Another favourite with the Society's patrons is Mr. H. M. Ashtm, who always infeses an irresistible humour into his delineations which makes them the more acceptable. As Noah Topping he presents a capital picture of a cooatry constable ailed with an all-pervading sense of his own import- ance and the :authority of his post He has, too, some advanced notions of" toreher " Which are interesting. Miss Lucy Wenham again contributes in no small degree to the success of the performances in the homely character of Hannah Topping, the constable's wife, her study Of the pirt being all that could be desired. A word of praise is due to Mr. R. P. Johnson who as Hatcham does what little is expected of him very. well.

Offensive Works at Penketh. At Warrington County Petty Sessions on Wednesday the Fleetwood. Chemical Company, Limited, were summoned for establishing an offensive works at Penketff without the consent of the District Council. Mr. Browne said the works were a. dreadful nuisance in the village, and the inhabitants had complained bitterly. The object of the Council was that the firm should give an undertaking that the ' works should be ' closed and the nuisance abated. Mr. M'Keever, tor the defendants, said the works had been closed and would not be re-opened,—A fine of 10s. was imposed,.

BEFORE THE MAGISTRATES.

WARNING TO PAUPERS.

- Michael McDermott, an inmate of the Warrington Workhouse, appeared before the magistrates on Satur- day for refusing to perform his task.—Prisoner was ordered to break 5 cwt. of stones, be t broke only about 1 , cwt.—John Constable, the workhouse porter, said prisoner would not work outside the workhouse or in it. He was an able-bodied man, but had been coming into the union for three years off and on.—Mr. J. 10. Awbery, the workhouse master. said the guardians were deter- mined that men like the prisoner should not be allowed to remain in the "house."—" You will have to go where they a. ill make von work," said the chairman of the magistrates (Mr. .I. IV. Hayward) in sending prisoner to gaol for twenty-one days. THEFT OF A Ham. At Warrington on Monday John McKeown, 4, Friars- lane, was charged with stealing as ham value 55. 6d., from the shop door of Messrs. Lipton's shop.—The Caief Constable stated that the prisoner was seen making his way along Bridge with the ham in his possession. Not looking the class of man who would pur- chase a whole ham he was taken to Messrs. Lipton's shop where it was found that the ham had been stolen from the doorway. In these hungry times a ham hanging in a doorway was undoubtedly a tempation, although the prisoner did not require much tempting having spent five years in a reformatory and haring been three times convicted of theft. —Prisoner was sent to gaol for 14 days with hard labour.

POACHERS PROSECUTED. At the Daresbury Petty Sessions, on Tuesday, before Mr. William Long (presiding magistrate) and Mr. Roger C. Parr, George Ogden, 24, Bank-street, Warrington, 'was charged with trespassing in search of game in the day time on land in the occupation of Mr. Richard Horton, at Appleton, on October 21st.—He pleaded not guilty.—Mr. E. A. White, who prosecuted, said that on the day in question, Mr. Broady, a gamekeeper, saw the defendant and two other men " flying hares." Two were holding a net which they were drawing across Mr. Horton's flsld for the purpose of entrapping hares, and the third man was on the footpath, apparently keeping watcb.—Defendant, who said he did not go on to the field, was fined 20s. and costs and also the advocate's fee. Thomas Griffiths. George Ellis, and Charles Steel, wireworks employes. of \Carrington, were at the same Sessions fined 10q. and costs each for trespassing in search of conies on land in the occupation of Joseph and William Bradbury at Moore on October 7th. James Broadhurst and James Monks, Mersey-street, Warrington, pleaded guilty to trespassing on Mr. T. H. Lyon's land at Appleton. on October 18th, in search of conies.—Mr. White, who prosecuted, said the defend- ants were seen standing round a rabbit hole. They had a ferret and three rabbit purse nets. Broadhurst was fined 10s. and costs, and Monks, who had a record of previous convictions, 20s. and costs. William Smith, 40, Plumpton-street, Warrington, was fined DN, and costs for having been unlawfully in search of and in possession of game at Acton Grange.—Superintendent Ennion said ha was at Acton Grange early on the morning of November Ist, in consequence of duck robberies, when he heard somone walking over the railway bridge. He went up the embankment, and the defendant, who had a stick, cried out " Heigh up." He did not strike, how- ever. and his name and address were obtained, lie had in his possession seven rabbits, is net 70 yards long, and several pegs.—Defendant said he had been out of work for some time and was hard put to. lie had not done this before.

HARRIERS.

WARRINGTON A.C. v. NORTH MANCHESTER H.— These clubs met on Saturday last at Mickley. Trail was laid over eight miles of very heavy country. The home club turned out 12 runners and Warrington 19. The result was a win for the visitors, who had the first twelve borne. Placings —Warrington: J. Parsonage, 1; S. Parsonage, 2; H. K. Jepson, 3; W. C. Brereton, 4; G. A. Brereton. 5; total 15 points. Mr. George Antill, professionally known as the " Evening Shadow," whose performances as a contor- tionist have afforded amusement to thousands, has undergone an experience that not only kept him off the stage for nine weeks, but actually threatened to prevent him ever again following this means of employment. A " Brighton Standards" reporter interviewed Mr. Antill when on a recent visit to Brighton Alhambra. " Yes, I am pleased to say that my trouble is now all gone," said the contortionist, " and for my recovery from biliousness and piles I have bile beans alone to thank. Some years ago, I became a victim to bilious- ness end piles. No one who has seen a contortionist perform could fail to imagine something of the agony which accompanied my performances while suffering from the piles. I always felt languid and depressed. I was troubled with sick headaches, arid the piles caused terribly acute rains across the regions of the kidneys " Sometimes 'during my performances the pains would catch me suddenly, 60 that I hardly knew how to get off the stage without collapsing. At last I became so weak and ill that I had to give up my profession, and for nin • weeks I was medically attended. On my return to the music halls, however, the old trouble returned, and I was forced to the conclusion that there was nothing for it but to leave the profession in which I had been so successfully engaged for some twenty years. However, I determined not to give in without a struggle. I tried almost every kind of medicine with- out gaining anything more than temporary relief, till a fellow artiste prevailed upon me to give bile beans a trial. The benefit I derived from the beaus was almost immediate.

"The heavy depressed feelings left ma, the bilious- ness departed entirely, and the pain from the piles gradually grew less severe. Finally, after a thorough Icourse of bile beans, I was completely cured." Bile beans cure biliousness, indigestion, constipation, piles, . anwinia," nerves," female ailments, weakness, dizziness, i rheumatism, pains in the back and side, insomnia, loss lof appetite, congested liver, headache, flatulence, !pimples and other eruptions and ailments having a 1 common origin in impaired action of the stomach and 1 liver, and are also effectual in warding off colds, chills, i neuralgia, and influenza. Of all chemists, or post free 1 from the bile bean manufacturing co., Red Cross-street, , London, E.C., on receipt of prices, one-and-three-half- pence or two-and-ninepence (two-and-ninepenny box contains three times the one-and-three-halfpenny size.) Do not accept substitutes.

GAIMEN SOCIETY.—The second monthly meeting of the members was held on Tuesday evening at the Church Rooms, Victoria-square when an excellent paper on " Roses" was read by Mr. J. Ryan followed by an interesting and instructive discussion. A small exhibi- tion of plants was held and in this connection the mem- bers were urged to make the future exhibits at these monthly meetings larger. It was announced that marks would be given each month to the exhibitors and prizes awarded to the three obtaining the highest number of marks at the end of the session. It is also the intention of the Society to hold a bulb show in March next.

The increasing popularity of the popular penny concerts held in Bold-street Wesleyan Schools was evideaced by the very large attendance at the fourth of the Keries which took place on S iturday evening. The room was almost filled and the admirable pro- gramme was highly appreciated. Songs were well rendered by Mrs. Dolan and Mr. Gittens and a duet by Miss Dunn and Master Tanner was well recei--ed. Uessrs. Crostield's prize baud quar- tette was in attendance and (b lighted the audi- ence with selections. Mr. Bedforth gave a euphonium solo. An exhibition of living pictures by Mr. Clinton Lewis added much to the attractiveness of the entertainment. •The chairman was Mr. J. [peon, who delivered an address on charity of judg- ment, askin,-, 1116 hearzrs always ta look for the good points in others, and not to be ever trying to find faul t

not be inclined to dispute. What is our remedy for this ?—to use to the fullest the brain power of the country, to leave none of it undeveloped or unexploited. (Applause). Take the example of Germany. Here, in spite of the way in which, as I have shown, they are hampered by Protection they have made enormous progress, and that, I beg you to observe, not in the protected. industries but particularly in those which require the greatest amount of brain Why have they as is admitted gone so far ahead of us in these things. The answer is, by their splendideduca- tiontal system; a system, mark you, which doss sot leave the boy until he has become a man and is practi- cally continued for all classes until each man has passed through the army. Professor Dewar estimates that three generations will be needed to overtake the start Germany now has in education; but, of course, he is reckoning upon the present state of progress. laly own •idea is that there is a remedy to hand that will bring us much earlier abreast of the Continent. I know that here lam treading on delicate ground ; but, perhaps, those who have followed me patiently so far will allow me, though they may differ from me in y conclusion, to say that in my opinion there is only one way, that of having a great national school, a school of the people to train them not merely when they are boys, but when they are men,from 18 to 20. In no other way it seems , to me, but by some measure, not necessarily oppressive, of military service, can the whole population be given I that training in clean. healthy, active and well ordered living, in method and the intelligent application of means to ends, which is got by passing through an insti- tution organized on some such lines as we see in the modern army. (Applause). The Chairman was thanked for his services, on the motion of Mr. ROBERTS, seconded by the Rev. J. DAVIES.

In responding, Mr. GREENING said he wanted the Free Trade movement to go. He knew no party politics where the question was before them as to whether they should have Protection or Free Trade, and his sole object was to help to retain Free Trade. (Applause.)

WYCLIFF E MISSIONARY ANNIVERSARY.

Rev. John Yonge's Appeal. Foreign missionary anniversary services were held at Wycliffe Church on Sudsy, the offertories being on behalf of the London Missionary Society. At the morning service the Rev. John Yonge preached from the words" Go ye, therefore, and teach all nations." They could not, he said, discover any more permanently and supremely fitting text for a missionary Sunda' than this original Word of the Lord which called missions into being. The Church of Christ had no choice but to obey His command or to prove recreaut and disloyal to the supremest Christian obligation. Continuing he pointed out that the churches which had cared most for the Christianising of the world hnd prospered in their own Christian lire, whereas those which had been supremely self-centred and self-absorbed had been the least vigor- ous and prosperous. Mr. Yonge dealt at some length with the great work which has been accomplished by the London Missionary Society in China, the South Seas, and Madagascar. The directors of the society were, however, unable to deal successfully with this progress. They were powerless to cope with the demands. Widening fields called for an augmented staff of missionaries, but the Society could not send them because the churches upon which it was dependent, did not furnish the missionary funds. The peril which faced the Society was very serious and very imminent, for unless the churches supplied before the end of 1903 considerably increased funds, the Society must , withdraw a considerable number of missionaries and stop altogether a very considerable part of its opera- tions. Unless £16,000 was in some way guaranteed by the Congregational Churches in the kingdom by the end of the present year the work must be curtailed. Having sketched the scheme which had been suggested for the raising of this sum, the preacher appealed to his hearers to do their utmost to increase the contributions from Wycliffe, and thus stimulate the great forward move- ment which the oven doors in all the mission fields encouraged the churches to make. In the evening the Rev. W. W. Stephenson preached on the work in India.

PUBLIC MEETING. THE NECESSITY FOR INCREASED SUPPORT.

lIIMMIMIII‘

pointed out that about fifteen years ago a Hindoo tract and Preaching Society were formed to combat Christianity but as its only methods were those of abuse it proved an utter failure. The people were beginning to show disrespect to their idols and they were not so devoted in keeping the festivals. Caste, which was one of the great difficulties and permeated everything, was losing its hold. Hindooism was a hoary system and was fast bound together. It would have to dissolve some day but it would probably be a long time. Till the Church felt its responsibilities he did not think the work would get very much farther. (Applause). The CHAIRMAN having thanked the speakers, said Mr. Arthur Dignum, formerly of Warrington, had resigned his position in the London Missionary Society but he was about to join the Society for the Propaga- tion of the Gospel.

WARRINGTON BOARD OF GUARDIANS.

The Position of the Medical Officer.

A meeting of the Warrington Board of Guardian; was held at the workhouse on Wednesday, Mr. J. Billinge presiding. There were also present :—Mrs. Clay, Mrs. Wilkinson, Miss Broadbent, Rev. Dr. Mercer' Messrs. W. Hickman (vice-chairman), A. J. Jolley, R.Foeshaw, J. W. Knowles, S. Davies, T. H. Ashcroft, G. Thomp- son, A. Brooks, W. Backhouse, W. Houghton, G. Mather, M. W. Seton, T. Ritchie, R. Flannagan, J. C. Sutton (clerk), J. F. Awbery (master), and A. Wright (superintendent of Padgate Schools).

MOUSE COM mrrriE. TICE POSITION OF THE MEDICAL OFFICER.

/ At a meeting of the House Committee on November I3rd a letter was read from the Local Government Board relative to the compensation inquiries forwarded to them in respect to Dr. Braide's appointment as work- house medical officer in the event of that appointment being disturbedibythe introduction of a resident medical officer': It was resolved to consider the matter that day week, and in the meantime Messrs. Billinge and Forshaw were appointed to interview Dr. Braide as a preliminary step.—On November 10th, another meet- ing was held when the Master called attention to Certain repairs to rtbe premises of the Workhouse. Hewes authorised to have carried out the minor repairs, but with regard to the building of the front wall of the vagrant wards, he was directed to ask Mr. William Owen to examine the same and report to the next meet- ing of the committee.—Probationer Nurse McNeil intimated to the committee that her three years' agree- ment expired on November 26th, on which day she would leave the service of the Guardians, and it was resolved that Sarah Holden, Padgate-lane, be appointed probationer in her place upon the usual terms and con - ditions. The Master reported the death of an unknown man who was brought in by the police on September 20th,and in respeet to whose name no information had been ascertainable.

In moving the adoption of the minutes Mr. FoftsrlAw said the committee were in communication with Dr. Braide and as soon as they were in a position to put definite proposals before the Board they would do so. Mr. THOMPSON seconded and the motion was carried.

PADGATE COTTAGE HOMES.

At a meeting of the Padgate Cottage Homes Com- mittee, it was resolved that an expression of sympathy with Mr. Payton upon his recent berearementgin the loss of his daughter be conveyed to hire by the Superintendent.—lt was also resolved that the Superin- tendent be impowered to engage a temporary assistant in No. 4 home for two weeks at a remuneration of 12s. a week and rations. It was further decided that the usual Christmas treat be allowed to the children and that £7 10s. be allowed for the purchase of prizes to be awarded as in previous years. The Superintendent reported upon the recent painting and enumerated certain internal work which had been postponed, and it was decided to defer any further decorating by outside labour until near the autumn.

The minutes were approved on the motion of Mr. MATHER seconded by Mrs. CLAY.

NEW LOCAL GOVERNMENT BOARD INSPECTOR.

Mr. 3 ENNK-It- FasT wrote informing the Board that Mr. William Pritchard Elias, of Bangor, had been appointed to succeed Mr. Moorsome as assistant inspector in that district. He ventured to bespeik for him the same kind consideration that the Board always extended to the representatives of the Local Govern- ment Board.

A public meeting was held in the Memorial Hall on Tuesday evening, the Rev. John Yonge presiding ever a fair attendance. The speakers were Mr. Stoveell Ashwell, a mission worker from Madagascar, and the Rev. W. W. Stephenson. The CHAIM:IAN said be confessed to some disappoint- ment that there was not a larger attendance. The society which they supported was the London Missionary Society and he found that according to the ennual report the amount sent from Wycliffe during last year was £B4 odd which included : Church collections, 230 • week night meeting, £e • Sunday school, £23 17s. 'lt had been announced that unles, the churches of the country could see their way to combine to produce £16,000 a year more than they had been doing recently, the Society would be driven to the painful necessity of withdrawing missionaries from their stations and putting an end to mission work which at present. was being carried on. A certain number of responses were being received which encouraged one to hope that the , churches were awakening to the necessity of laying upon themselves more largely the order of self-sacrifice for the missionary cause. He hoped the people of Wycliffe would do their part in the matter. There were a number of claims pressing heavily upon them at present but notwithstanding that he was sanguine enough to hope that they would be able to increase their contribution to the society very considerably during the epming year. Madagascar was a great missionary field with whose ' history they had been familiar for a long time and in which they had always felt a deep and sympathetic interest. Whatever might happen to the London His- sionary Society, they hoped it would be one of the last things dreamed of that there should be any diminution of its interest or labours in that important field which had been so much their own. A writer in one of the denominational papers said he was strongly of opinion that unless the churches responded to the cry for more help the great work of the Society ia Madagascar would have to come to an end. Such an event would be asource of unspeakable sorrow to all Congregationalists—in fact it would be a scandal and a shame. He could not for a moment believe that that would happ en. Mr. STOWELL AsliwsrA. gave an interesting descrip- ticin of the work which is being done in Madagascar. He pointed out that the island was three or four times as large as the United Kingdom but the population was only about four and a half millions. The policy of the London Missionary Society bad not been to work only on the coast, but they bad gone straightaway to the capital. The people believed in witchcraft very strongly indeed and in the olden times the system of trial by ordeal" was in force. Another belief was that the departed spirits hovered 'round the tombs and villages and in consequent:* the people spent a great deal more money in electine handaome tombs than in building decent houses. The mission bad been a very successful one and there were a great number of people who were striving earnestly to serve the Lewd. They had 355 churohes and about 20,000 chdrch members and nearly 100,030 adherents in the central portion of the island. There were,howeyer only fifteen missionaries and one missionary some times had charge of as many as forty churches. All the churches had native pastors who were elected by the people. The society had established in the capital a large high school for boys, two high schools-for girls, a training college, a printing office. and flee memorial churches. Their object was first to train the young men in the schools and afterwards in the college that they might go out as evangelists to preach to their own countrymen. At the present time the relationship between the English missionaries in Madagascar and the French Government was a very happy one, and they received just the same privileges that were allowed to French missionaries. (Applause). The Rev, W. W. STEPIIRN&)N reforrei to the work in India. His own district, he said, was about three times the size of Lancashire and there were only two mission- aries to work it. Before they could do much preaching they had to win the confidence of the people. The Hindoos were very selfish and very suspicious of th motives of everybody. In every way that was honour- ' Sixpennyworth of bichromate of potassium was sal- able the missionaries had to try and win their confidence cient to make 200 bottles of a liquid which Robert and to make them realize that they wanted to be their Pickering, of Chester-street, Blackburn, was charged friends. He thought the people had now begun to with selling as a lacquer. Two months'imprisonruent realize that and to trust the missionaries more than was meted out to him, they trusted anyone else. isn.t an allegory, nor a fair story, and .i.sn't any special moral. The only moral is .t it is true.

Well, I went one year to stay with a friend in Canada. My friend had a farm called Swampscot, near Collingwood, a little town at the head of Lake Superior, the station whence the 'steamers start for their trip through the lake and to the far North-west.

There was quite a number of wolves is that region, when my friend first . went there tt>. ; but he had a number of ddgs on the farm, and some , of them were very fierce, and strong; so, after a few years, during which the dogs and the wolves often met, the wolves found it was hardly worth their while to pay a visit to Mr. Noble—who was the owner of Swampscot Farm —because one of those dogs would undoubtedly be disagreeable enough to bark at them, 'and then in a moment the whole pack would come tumbling out, and the boys would run helter- skelter to see the fun, and away would! go Mr. Wolf, With such a Shoutino•° and hallooing and barking at his heels that he would think the end of the world was at hand And very often indeed it was, so. Jar as he was concerned, thul he might consider himself lucky if he could reach the safe shelter of the big woods which came down to the edge of Mr. Noble's clearing. For more than once it happened that old Jowler came sauntering back 'to the house with a grim. look, which said just as plainly as if he ,could talk, " There's 'another of those rascals out. Of the way." And, soon the. boys would come run- ning in, with the v,.olt's head to nail up, on tim barn,door ; nod that Was the concluSion of little visit ti Swampscot.

So, yon 'see, it didn't pay the wolves to coni7 and see U 3 on ordinarr occasions. Only when the little new-horn lambs were out in the fields with their raotlien,, Would a wolf now and then find an opporruni;y to nap up one of the babies and, carry it If to his family in the forest. Oh, you 'thought I was only in fun, did you, and, that I meant to tell you about a 'wolf chasing a sheep, after all? Just wait a moment till I tell my sLory. So far I have written only what the story-book pe3ple call an introductory chapter.

Now, the year I was E:t Swainpscot, it hap- pened .that Mr. Noble's little daughter Annie, a dear little girl with rosy cheeks and curly yellow locks, took a •treat fancy to have one of the lambs for a pet. 53 her father had one of the little white, fluffy baby-lambs brought intd the house, and • Annie used to feed it and carry it about in her arms as if it were a little toy animal. •

However, the lamb soon grew too heavy -to be carried around by so small a girl, and before •summer had well begun it was trotting about everywhere after Annie, like Mary's 'little lamb in that poem which some of you may have heard. All the dogs were pets of Annie's, as indeed were all the animals in the farmyard; and at first, when she took her baby sheep out in the yard, the dogs did not know what to make of it. They wagged their tails and barked " how-odo- you-cld " to Annie's new friend; but the poor little lambkin did not understand the dog lan- guage ,at all, and ran frightened to Annie, and hid its little head in her, dress.

" Littlo 80-peep has lost hcr Sheep."

There was a little ,shepherdess, 80-peep, A rustic -maiden, with a little crook,' Blue gown, and while straw hat, and she did look Fresh as the dawn, and 80-peep loved her sheep. Nevertheless one day she fell asleep, Hidden away from sight in a green nook, Her hat thrown off, but in her hand her hook. She woke, looked round, and then began to weep; t Her lambs werc gone! She strained her eyes

• to get A glimpse of their retreating tails, and then She ran and Shouted. Not one did appear. Sit down; dear little girl, and never fret; Leave them alone and they'll come back again, Bringing their tails behind thorn,„ never fear.

DECENBER: • Decembers come, and With- her brought A world in whitest marble wrought ; The trees, and. fence, and all the posts Stand motionless and white as ghosts, And all the paths we used to know Are hidden in the drifts of snow. December brings the longest night And cheats the day of half its light. No bird-song breaks the perfect hush; No meadow-brook with liquid gush Runs telling tales in babbling rhyme Of liberty and summer-time, But frozen in its icy cell Awaits the sun to .break the spell. • Breathe once upon the window-glass And see tlte mimic mists that pass,— Fantastic shapes .that go and come For ever silvery and dumb. December Santa Claus shall bring,— Of happy children • happy king, Who with his sleigh and reindeer stops At all good people's chimney-tops. Then let the holly red be hung, And sweetest carols all be sung, While we with joy remember them,— The journeyers to Bethlehem, Who followed trusting from afar The guidance of that happy star Which marked the spot where Christ was born Long year's ago one Christmas morn! • 0 THE TABLES TURNED: (A Wolf-Story Reversed.) • Did you ever hear of a sheep chasing a Wolf? A WOMAN WHO MIGHT HAVE BEEN QUEEN,

A. F. S'FETLtItT (.4,,t1,0r cf" Tlie Fonndrrs of Penang and A dcla:t?e,"

Lady Sarah Lennox was the fourth daughter of Charles, second Duke of Richmond mid Len- MX in Britain„ mid Due d'Aubigny in France, a • Duchy which be inherited from his grandmother Louise de la Kerounille, the Bretonne enchant- ress of his grandfather, King Charles 11. She was born to the purple, was young Lady i Sarah, in 1745, not one of her family was out- side the "charmed elide." Her eldest brother was Charles, Third , Duke of Richmond. Her married sisters were Caroline, Lady Holland ; Emily, Lady Kildare (Duchess of Leinster later) and Lady Louisa Conolly, the " Providence" of the whole family \ and her aunts and cousins members of the Albemarle, Berkeley, and other noble houses—and this knowledge, while it did not spoil, could not help affecting her through life.

In early youth she was the child favourite of the old King George H., who spoiled her, as every old man would spoil a lovely little girl, and idolised by all her relatives as the beauty of a beautiful family, she grew up in a strange en. vironnient of admiration.

Bu it was not unmerited. At the age of 15. the young King George 111, marked her re- markable beauty, and himself the son of the un- popular Augusta, Princess of Wales, a Princess of Saxe-Coburg, thought—if only for a moment —of her as an English queen.

A ROYAL PROPOSAL.

The young girl was amused. by his attention, her relations, Lord Holland, the Duke of Bich- mond and Lord Ilchester, intensely anxious, her bosom friend, Lady Susan Strangeways, eager, for, as she wrote later, "I was almost believed myself Prime Minister." The King's admira- tion of the young gal was genuine. Lord Hol- land saw it and made Lady Sarah take -pleasure in the haymaking at Holland Park when the King was passing. The King fell into the trap, and grew more and more in love with her. The King first laid his proposal at her feet through her friend Lady Susan, and when she had told him she intended to stay in London until after his coronation, he answered " But there will be no coronation until there is a Queen, and I think your friend is the fittest per- son for it, tell your friend so from me." Later he approached Lady Sarah herself, and asked her if her friend had told her what he had said. "Yes, Sir." "And what do you think .1)? ? Tell me for my happiness depends on it !" " Nothing, Sir," said the frightened girl, and the King left her in a huff. That be would really have married her is not at all improbable, but during the courtship (of which Lord Hol- land preserved a careful record) Lady Sarah broke her leg when out riding, and was laid up in the country, and also indulged in a boy and girl flirtation with young Lord Newbattle. Yet when she returned to Court the King " had no eyes but for her," and talked to her as much as his position allowed, and even on June 18th said to her, " For God's sake remember what 1 bald to Lady Susan before you went to the country, and believe I have the strongest at- tachment."

===== 0002601_19051118_art0091.txt | included=true | included =====
TFIE PHILOSOPHY OF LADY SAUAH.

Yet on 'Txly Bth he announced to his Connell that he was engaged to marry Prin- cess Charlotte of Mecklenburgh-Strelitz ! Doubt- less reasons of State induced this step, as his admiration for Lady Sarah was sincere, and he spoke of her great beauty and charm until his death. Lord Holland continues naively : "To many a. girl ILII.'s behaviour had teen very vexatious, but Lady Sarah's temper and affec- tions are happily so flexible and light that the sickness of her squirrel immediately took up all her attention, and when it, in spite of her nusing, dy'd, I believe it gave her more concern than H.M. ever did. That grief, however, soon gave way to the 'care of a little hedge-hog, that she say'd from destruction in the . field, and is now her favourite." She showed her spirit too in her letter to her friend. " I have almost for- given him ;' luckily for me I did not love him, and only liked him, nor did the title weigh any- thing with me . . . I did not my, I assure you, which I believe you will, as you were more set upon it than I was. The thing lam most angry at is looking so like a fool " . . . and again " must go to the first Drawing ltoom, shall not trouble them with my company until then ; I wonder if they will name me train-bearer, I wish they would, tho' they abuse me and call me names, for I think it's the best way of see- ing the coronation." This was wonderful strength of character for so young a girl, and after all she did go, and took the place of chief bridesmaid • ''Chief Angel," Horace Walpole wrote ; so diat the King, as Lord Holland teasing- ly said, "shall see your pretty face mid re- pent." What gars rise to some amusement and got-sip was that she was mistaken for the new Queen by the purblind old Lord Westmoreland, who, after the ceremony, dropped on his knees before the startled girl, and took her hand to kiss!

A YOUNG WOMAN OF STRONG CHARACTER.

, Lady Sarah herself married soon after on June 2nd, 1762, Sir Thomas Charles Danbury, of Ilan, ton, a handsome young sportsman, and a Suf.,. folk squire " a match of her own making." of her doings as his somewhat neglected and very beautiful wife one need say little, but after a brilliant society career, she put an end to her 1 happiness in 1769, by eloping with Lord William Gordon. _ -

She left him soon, and retired into private life under the wing of her brother, the Duke of Richmond, and in great privacy, educated her only daughter, Louisa Bunbury, and, not un- conscious of her fault, refused to be received by any of her old society friends save " as a fav- our." She remained thus at Goodwood near her brother (her divorce bad been passed in 1776) and there were rumours that Sir Charles Bun- bury desired to re-marry her. She refused this, however, though she continued friendly to him and to his sisters, and remained living quietly, and as she said " very dignified." But in 1776, there appeared Col. the Hon. George Napier, a handsome son of Lord Napier and Ettriek, a widower, in her brother's regi- ment. He wooed her, and she fell in love with him, and at last, in spite of all opposition, mar- ried him on August 27th, 1781, and never was an unpropitious marriage so wonderfully success- ful. Her husband until his death hi. 1804, adored her, and her sons, " the heroic Napiers " worshipped her.

Though blindness and comparative poverty overtook her, she did not lose her spirit ; she wrote of her blindness that she was not much consoled by being told that she had a " beautiful cataract just like the King's," for her old admirer bad become blind also. She held a unique position in the world, until her death on August 26th, 1826, as everyone knew that she‘ might have reigned over Britain, the Prince of Wales (George IV.) pointing to Wind- sor Cantle, said to Lady Celicia Johnstone, " She should have been there." THE MAINSTAY OP LIFE "Added to all floods raises the nutritive value enormouslar."—Lanot.

TflE WAho Of TUB WORLD.

}bit oh, child century, what 6111111 be thy re- d in matters far weightier than revolutions 01 literary taste ? Thou art born into a world armed to the teeth, groaning under immense armaments, which make all schemes of imlwove- ment 'hopeless. And yet there is hardly an un- settled question not to be solved by a stroke of the pen. And in spite. of ail this ruinous cost, the cry of suffering and ontrage arise continually in onr midst, while irresistible forcer., paralysed by mutual jealousies, only keep the ring for the 'Or- derer. The husbands and tethers, nay the wouleik and little children perish on the wintry Mace- donian or Armenian mountains, homeless and starving. But no hand is r:ti,•ed to help them. Presently, as so often before, order will be declared to be restored, and the old cycle ,or wrongdoing, oppression and revolt will commence again once more. But is it for 01100 only or for ever ? And in the Far East a gigantic war has suddenly sprung up —a conflict of races which must recast the future of the world. Is the Oriental, arnied with the terrible weapons and all the intelligence of the West, to drill millions of disciplined semi-savages of kindred nee... for whom death hits no more terrors than it had for the hordes who blotted out Rome, to sweep away • our religion and our hope ? Or will the North triumph, and later absorb our Western into its own lower type, or yith its wider empire haPpily full by its own weight, till the iron despotism which now crushes the individual, is replaced by groups of self-governed Republics, with the freedom and. progress of youth ? who live now are confronted by tremen- dous problems, and have more need of faith in an over-ruling purpose for good than men have ever bad before since the fall of Rome.

MAYOR'S SUNDAY.

Official Visit to the Warrington Parish Church. •

In accordance with a time-honoured custom the Mayor and Corporation attended Divine service at the War- rington Parish Church on Sunday morning as a fitting way of inaugurating the municipal year. Unfortunately wretched weather prevailed and the attendance suffered in consequence. A procession was formed at the Town Hall at ten o'clock, headed by the Volunteer Band. A large muster of Volunteers had paraded at the Drill Hall, but shortly before the time for leaving, rain fell so heavily that at the request of the Mayer the men were dismissed. The following officers joined in the procession :—Majors J. D. Fairclough, B. Fairclough, F. M. Appleton ' • Captain's R. Smith, and P.N. Avery ; Lieutenants, H. Owen, R. H. Lepage, J. T. Picton and .1. W. Boumphrey and Captain and Quartermaster A.F.G Potter. • A posse of police under the direction of Chief Inspec- tor Phillips was in attendance, the Fire Brigade, in charge of Superintendent Turner and deputy Super- intendent Turner, and the Ambulance Corp; under Captain Waring also paraded. The Mayor, who was preceded by the mace and sword bearers, was accom- panied by the deputy Mayor (Alderman Faiiclough).and the Town Clerk (Mr. J. Lyon Whittle). The other members of the Corporation present were Aldermen Smethurst, Bennett, Pierpoint and Tinnion, Councillors Roberts, Evans, Wilkinson,Craik, Clegg, Sankey, Wilson, Burton. Barton, Ellison, Skelland, Fulton Smith, Ash- croft, Dickenson, Wright and Dr. Joseph. The officials in attendance were Messrs. J. Fairhurst, (borough treasurer),W. H. Woodcock, (borough collector), F. W. Timperley (borough treasurer's department), T. Longdin (borough surveyor), A. M. Kerr (assistant borough surveyor). T. H. Spinks (assistant to the Town Clerk), J. G. Gornall (medical officer of health), W. T. Flood, (inspector of nuisances). F. G. Ruddock (borough analyst), J. Moore Murray (director of education), C. Msdeley (curator and librarian), C. B. Aylward (headmaster School of Art), W. S. Haddock (gas engineer), F. Taylor (secretary of the Gas Department), E. Place (inspector of weights and measures). Others present included Messrs. R. T. Fairclough, J. W. Hayward, A. J. Jolley, S. Wright, C. S. Burgess (magistrates), G. Walker, W. Bibby (Educa- tion Committee), S. Suttou, Sidney, Frank and Samuel bolton, G. Wallington' W. Rogers, A. Jolley, S. Davies, H. Owen, H. Isherwood, S. Taylor, and F. Plimpton. The processionists had only gone a a short distance along Sankey-street when rain again !fell in torrents and cabs were brought into use. On arrival at the Parish Church the Mayor and Corporation were met by the clergy and choir who preceded them up the centre aisle. The usual office of Morning Prayer was gone through, the prayers being intoned by the Revs. G. A. Dearden and T. W. Barber. The lessons were read by the Revs. T. A. J. Ridpath and E. J. VVillcocks. The choir rendered the musical part of the service in a highly creditable manner, Mr. F. M. Darby, Mus. Bac, presiding at the organ. The anthem was lam Alpha and Omega, the beginning and the end, saith the Lord." The preacher was the Rev. IW. Mower Smith, rector of Walkden, near Manchester, who took for his text the words "It came even to pass, as the trumpeters and singers were as one, to make one sound'to be heard in praising and thanking the Lord ; and when they lifted up their voice with the trumpets and cymbals and instruments of music, and praised the Lord saying For He is good ; for His mercy endured for ever: that then the house was filled with a cloud, even the house of the Lord; so that the priests could not stand to minister by reason of the chud : for the glory of the Lord had tilled the house of God." He spoke of the dedication of the Temple as being in many respects the very greatest day in the, history of the kingdom of Israel, the very acme of their glory. One of the lessons which he drew from the text was that our worship if the spirit of God, was to come down and fill His temples now as He filled them long ago, must. be onei How wretched was the religion how wretched was the worship of God when unity was gone I The Holy Ghost had gone and all energy and enthusiasm had gone with Him. Might all those present, said thepreacher, be receivers and dispensers of the 'Holy Spirit. He concluded by appealing for liberal contributions to the offertory, which was on behalf of the choir fund, pointing out what an important part the choir played in leading the worship of God. At the conchision of the service the National Anthem was sung. The return of the procession to the Town Hall was witnessed by a large number of townspeople the rain having ceased.

NORWAY WANTS A KING. -- Nearly 200,000 Majority in Favour of

Prince Charles.

Prince Charles of Denmark appears assured by a large majority of an invitation to occupy the throne or Nor- way. The latest return in the plebiscite areas follow:— For Against The figures are now outstanding from only eighteen districts. After the announcement of the preliminary result of the plebiscite in favour of the election of Prince Charles a large crowd gathered in front of the Premier's residence .and made a demonstration ia favour of the Govern- ment. Prince and Princess Charles will leave Copenhagen on the 23rd inst. for Christiania to take up their new position as King and Queen of Norway.

LIBERAL ACTIVITY.

ENTHUSIASTIC STOCKTON HEATH

fIIEETING.

The Failures e th 3 Present Government. --- RETALIATION FALLACIES EXPOSED.

LAND !V' ATIONALIZATION.

After a somewhat lengthy period of inaction the Stockton Heath Liberal Association is again affording evidence that it will be a power to be reckoned with in the coming general election. On Tuesday evening a large and enthusiastic meeting was held in the Hall Cafe, Assembly room. Mr. Sherburn, president •of the association, occupied the chair, and was sup- ported by Mr. A. J. King, the prospective Liberal candididate for the Knutsford divisim, Dr. Hodgson, Crewe, vice-chairman of the Cheshire County Council Education; Committee, Messrs. H. Roberts, W. Madden, Josiah Longland, E. Lloyd Jones, and Joseph Smith and the Rev. J. S. Hughes.

THE GENERAL ELECTION. The Chairman, at the outset, referred to the loss which the Liberal Association had suffered by the death of Mr. Mounfield, who was their chairman, and Mr. A. H. Dunn, who discharged the duties of hon. treasurer. The best way in which they could show their apprecia- tion of the work of the two gentlemen was:by closing up their ranks as quickly as posaible.(Hear,hear.)Mr.Balfour had told them that the dissolution would probably take place at no distant date. The issues of the General Election for which they had so long been waiting, would be very serious. In that fight they wanted to give their opponents credit for being actuated by motives as sincere as theirs were, with the vital difference that they viewed things from a different stand- point. The point of view of the Tory party, judged from their legislation, was one of privileges, while that of the Liberal Party was public control of public matte's. (Applause.) They had one or two other questions which would have to be thrashed out at the coming election. One was the education enestion.Speaking recently at Warrington the Bishop of Manchester pointed out some of the advant- ages which the present Act presented as compared with the old School Boards, but he seglected to point out that while the old School Boards were directly elected by the people the present education authorities were not. Never in the history of any Government had there been shown the same tendency of legislation to take the control away from the great masses of tho population. On the temperance question there were on all hands bitter complaints of the power having been taken out of the hands of the licensing magistrates. There were also the fiscal question and the question of Chinese labour which bad to be fought and on all these matters it would be their endeavour to conduct the fight in a kindly manner. (Applause.) SPEECH BY MR. KING. Mr. Ring, who had a flattering reception, said that was the fifth year of their campaign in the Knutsford division, and he thought they had every reason to feel encouraged at the progress which they had made. It was started in the darkest days of Liberalism more as a protest and to relieve their consciences, without any expectation of making any impression upon the Tory majority in that division. It now appeared to him that if they iveut into the election with the determination to make any sacrifices which were necessary there was every prospect that they might at length change the repre- sentative of the division. (Applause). One was almost ashamecl,he continued,to hit a man when he was down and surely if ever a government was down it was the present government which had sunk very low in the estimation and respect of the countrylt was not down in the sense that it had lost its majority and that it was out. of office and it was therefore their duty ta bring it down in those directions. (Applause.) Mr. King briefly touched upon the war in South Africa. We were told that South Africa after the war would enjoy a period of prosperty such as it had never known before, and would be open for the surplus population of England. \Vhat a miser- able picture the country presented to-day--a picture of ruin and desolation, the country given over to the min- ing magnates to run for their personal profit by Chinese slaves. In 1895 we were told that the Tory Party was the party of social reform and peace. We had had very littlo from them in the way of social reform and had had a great deal of trouble in all parts of the world. Mr. Balfour claimed that the present Government had maintained the peace of the world and had carried progressive and social reform measures. A more barefaced statement could not be made by any responsible statesman. Let them consider the Government as financiers and economists. During the last ten years the national expenditure had gone up by £65,000,000 per annum, The Government was supposed to be placing on one side an annual sum of money with -the object of reducing the national debt. In reality we were not reducing the national debt at all and would not under present arrangement, be able to do so for many _ rears to come. Let them consider the Government as administrators. What about the Army. (Applause.) We were told that the REPORT OF THE BUTLER COMMISSION. was out of date. People might forget it but they must be reminded that the army administrators in South Africa sold goods for us, and bought them back off the same contractors for 17s. 101. and moreover paid the 17s. 10d. before they got the Its. In 1900 the Govern- ment proposed to re-organize the army. Since then we bad had three army schemes and yet Lord Roberts said the other day that the army was as ill-prepared and equipped as it was before the South African war. (Applause). As the result of the administration of the present government we had the lamentable spectacle of ' a bitter controversy between two high officials the' result of which was that the population of India had been handed over to a military dictatorship, the , control of the Viceroy having practically been done away with. We were responsible for the welfare 'of the people of India and surely them must be something wrong when the people of that country were dying in such large numbers from starva- tion and famine. (Applause). If there was one thing for which the Tory p,rty boasted that they were sticklers, it was constitution, alas/ yet the Tory Govern- ment had reduced the House of 'Commons to a position of helplessness, and had arrogated to itself the right to say what subjects should be discussed in Parliament. One of the most startlingly unconstitutional incidents on record was the reply of Mr. Balfour to the complaints of the action of the mob in wrecking the property of the Itowntree family because they invited a deputation of Boers to address a meet- ing. Mr. Balfour said "There is a limit to human endurance." It would be very daegerous to remind the deputation of starving women who waited on the Prime Minister, that in the words of Mr. Balfour himself there was a limit to human endurance—(appiause)— , after all he thought the present Government belonged to the history of the country. He did not think they would have much to do with its future and therefore it was important that they must learn ° WHAT TO EXPECT FROM A LIBERAL COVERNMEtsIT. ^, That Government would have great difficulties to face. They had an immense amount of work before them in simply undoing the mischief which the present Govern- scent had done. They would also have to turn their attention to some great and pressing social questions. There was the licensing question. Fhe power of the control of licenses must be given into the hands of the people. (Applause.) Then they must reduce expenditure. It seemed to him that some- thing might be done with regard to a reduction of the expenditure on the Army and Navy. He thought tho time was opportune when they could approach other Governments with a mutual scheme for the reduction of armaments. (Hear' hear.) Other great questions with which they would have to deal were the housing ques- tion, the land question, and the unemployed question. We were told that an alteration in our fiscal policy was necessary not only to maintain our present markets but to create new markets. What about the 12,000,000 people of England who were on the verge of starvation? Would it not be better to satisfy their demands by means of domestic legislation and so create a home market rather than attempt to create a market at a dis- tance? (Applause.) During the last 20 years some 1,009,000 labourers had left the land and come into our great cities and towns. If they could be got back to the land they would represent a population which would create a market. The question arose how were they to ibe got back ? In the first place they must be given some inducement. At the present time there was no inducement for the tenant farmer to remain on the land, as any increased profits which he made went into the pockets of his landlord in the shape of increased rent. The least that they ought to he given was a fair rent, fixity of tenure:and a free sale of improvements. (Applause.) Moreover they should be placed in such a position that they could not be turned off their farms for political or religious opinions. (Hear, hear.) The land question in the towns presented a different aspect and be personally believed that the only radical solution of the difficulty lay in

Big Corporations should be given -greatly increased powers to buy and hold land not only in the towns but on the borders of the towns so that any increased value created by the industry of the people should accrue to the people. (Applause.) The Liberal party was standing for great principles—for reform and advance, for liberty and freedom of the press and speech and freedom and equality of opporturnty—(hear,hear)— and for the development of our great Empire on the best and richest lines. (Applause). He moved in conclu- sion " That this meeting views with emphatic dis- approval the action of the present Government in using its majority to carry unpopular measures through Parliament without due consideration, and having forfeited the confidence of thi3 country as shown unmis- takably by recent bye-elections, this meeting declares that the only honourable course for a Government so I,4l.tufqed is the immediate resignation of office."

lApplause.) DR. HODGSON AND ELECTION. Dr. Hodgson seconded. One aspect of the legislation of the present Government, he said, was the rewards which they had given to their friends. In the act for , the extension of Local Goverrment in Ireland they

offered one of the biggest bribes that was ever offered to class interest. Prior to that Act the poor rate was paid equally by the landlord and the tenant and the county rate was paid entirely by the tenant. The Local Government Act of 18eS actually forgave the landlord his share in the poor rate. In the county rate . the tenant was relievea ollhalf of the ratelwas paid by the , State. By being relieved of the poor rate the Duke of I Devonshire was relieved of the payment of £B5B, Lord I Lansiown of IBS and the Marquis of Downshire of f,238. That aptly illustrated the principle of Tory legislation. Dealing with the Education Act he said 1 the Act was passed to relieve the"lntolerable burden " upon the supporters of voluntary schools but it had 1 failed in its object. The strain remained though in a diffrent manner. The rates in Cheshire had gone up led. in the Z. The County rate had not increased and the increase was due.to the rates for elementary ad higher education. That enormous increase should have been foreseen by the Government. It was a most iniquitous thing, he thought, if the Government did not know this, that they should enter lightly upon a thing which meant such a terrible increase without first of all ascertaining what was going to happen. (Applause) The explanation of the increase was that when tne local • authority came into existence it was discovered that many of the schools were in a disgraceful condition. In • fact, they might have declined to maintain a large num- bar of schools. They did not do so and endeavouring to administer the Act in the best interests of the people they called upon the school managers to make all sorts of improvements and alterations. The Act had also I necessitated large increases of the teaching staff, and , increases in salaries if they were to retain the teachers, and consequently instead of the Act being a relief as was intended it had become a burden. He believed. • however, that the whole of the difficulties with regard 4to the Act could be settled in a satisfactory manner I , such as would be received by everyone. There was a 1 • strong feeling in the minds of some people that the ; religious aspect of the question most be preserved, and when an Education Amendment Act was present Parliament would have to sea that such proposals were [made as would be likely to meet with general approval. • That could be done so long as there was a desire to do what was right. Religious toleration must not be read into the Act. There must be freedom and equality,and equality of treatment for teachers irrespective of i religious beliefs. With regard to THE LICENSING A.CT all that had been prophesied by those who opposed the Act had come to pass. In that Act also the Govern- ment had rewarded their friends. The compensation which was awarded did not go to the tenant except in a very meagre degree, lout went into the pockets of the landlords. We had had a startling illustration of the , difficulties of administering the Act. For many years 1 the Birkenhead licensing magistrates had made certain I verbal conditions upon the renewing of the licences. Upon the passing of the Act they insisted upon those conditions being put into writing. An appeal was entered and the 'magistrates, decision was upheld. In the Court of Appeal to which the case was next taken the decision of the magistrates was reversed, and they were saddled with the whole of the costs of the pro- ceedings. The Justices of the King's Bench division had upheld the decision of the magistrates, and why they should not have to bear part of the costs was one of the anomalies of the Licensing Act which must he removed. If magistrates were to be penalized in that way how could anyone get justice -in dealing with licensing administration? (Applause). We wanted a real Licensing Act which would give us some power over the granting or the removing of licenses, and which would give the holder of a license a direct interest in his business, and a chance of making a living in an honest manner. (Applause). The resolution was carried with enthusiasm, only four voting against it. IrilE FISCAL QUESTION. Mr. Henry Roberts, who had a hearty reception, at the outset associated himself with the expression of regret at the loss which the Association had suffered by the death of Mr. Mounfleld and Mr. Dunn. They ought he thought, to recognise the courtesy of the owner of those premises. When they remembered the difficulties with which Liberalism in Stockton Heath had been faced in respect to finding a place in which to hold meetings, it made one appreciate the great courtesy of the gentleman, opposed to them in politics, who had extended to them the hospitality which that room afforded. (Applause). He had not intended taking a very active part in the coming general elections but he had come forward because he had read the declarations of the Prime Minister as to the future policy of the Conservative Party—a policy of retalia- tion and colonial preference. He desired to say a word or two as to the effect of the policy upon industries in which be is personally interested. The first question he would ask a tariff reformer or a retaliator was " What are manufactured goods and what are raw materials." Up to that moment no satisfactory answer had been given to that question. Let them take THE OUESTION OF IRON RODS._

To firms in Warrington which manufactured them they were a manufactured article,to firms which used them they were raw material. How were we going to restrict by means of a tariff the importation of manufactured articles and at the same time allow the users to get iron rods free from taxes because to them they were raw material? Wire weaving in Warrington was largely done from iron rods which came from abroad. Put a tax upon iron rods the home manufacturer immediately takes advantage of the tax and up would go the price of that article. We could not stand a ten per cent tax upon iron rods at the present prices and at the same time maintain our trade in South Africa. He would endeavour to show how impossible it was to carry out the principle of colonial preference. Canada and the United States both sent us a large amount of wheat, and the Canadian grower would be given a preference over the American. During the winter months the Canadian ports were closed and Canadian produce had to be carried across America and shipped from a United States port. The Yankee was supposed to be about the cutest busi- ness man on earth, and the appelation could be justly given to him. Did they think he would allow Canadian produce to go across his railways well knowing that the Canadian grower had a preference over the grower in the United States? (Applause). It was utterly opposed to common sense to imagine for a moment that he would do so. (Applause). •For six years he had been associated with the soap trade. During the past 20 years the firm with which he had the honour to be connected, had developed its business and increased it tenfold, and that under a Free Trade policy. Practi- cally the whole soap for export trade of the world in free markets was produced in England, and to a very large extent by three Lancashire firms, because not a single continental marm- facturer could obtain the articles required in the manufacture of soap as cheaply as they could be obtained in England. If we gave the Canadian wood and pulp merchants the preference

of packing eases and piper. Those were, comparatively speaking, only small matters, but they would be suffi- cient to sweep away the margin of profit which enabled the English manufacturer to secure the trade. That was not all however. The Australian would come along and dsmand a preference upon mutton fat, over the beef fat which came from Argentina. lie was now speaking of something substantial as tallow entered largely into the manufacture of soap. There was not a single thing in connection with the trade from tallow to the nails or the boxes which would not go up in price therefore soap would be hit most seriously and heavily by any- thing in the shape of colonial preference and they were not going to have it. (Applause.) That was one of the things which made him come forward. The second was the shameful act of the present Government, at a time like the present, when the starving women of London went to see the Premier to ask for bread, to offer them something in the shape of a bastard Act of Parlia- ment, which was not worth the paper upon which it WAS written, called the Unemployed Act. The Government which poured out the blood of thousands of British soldiers and wantonly wasted millions of money to secure for us, he hoped for all time, the possession of territory in South Africa, refused bread to the people of our own land—this same Government which tried to exclude a few thousand aliens upon a most contemptible pretext refused to give bread to our own flesh and I blood and at the same time promoted the importation of yellow men into South Africa to take the place of white men. Any man in that room was in a false position as a citizen of England if he would overlook the impious crime which the British Government per- mitted by allowing the introduction of slavery into South Africa. He was false to the traditions of thenation, false to the position which he occupied and false to the men who had gone before him and given their lives for the emanicipation of our own people. (Applause.) When he thought of the starving men and women beingleft to the tender mercy of an act of Parliament which was absolutely useless passed by a Government which had promoted so wicked a thine.' as was to be seen in South Africa in order to fill the pockets, which were already bursting with wealth, obtained in a manner best left to themselves, of Jew financiers, he said it was time for every man with any spark of marlhood about him to pat all other considers- tions on one side and to vote and work for the right , man—Mr, King—at the next general election. 1 (Applause.) Hemoved a vote of thanks to Mr. King and Dr. Hodgson for their addresses. (Applause,) ADDRESS BY MR. MADDEN.

Mr. Madden, who was received with applause, said he I was pleased to be present because he was not merely 'a Liberal but a Radical of Radicals, and he hoped the next Government would be a Radical one. Referring to the Licensing question he said the solution of the difficulty lay very much in the direction of not allow- ing a brewer to hold more than one license, Only by ' that would they returu to the old days of the good old English landlord who was responsible for the good con- duct of his house. The people and the magistrates must have control of the drink traffic. At the present time it was not the licensing magistrates but the brewing interest which dominated. The magistrates were mere puppets if their decisions were to be upset as they bad been. (Applause). Mr. Chamber- lain had played a desperate game all his life. He had been playing a game to obtain the Premiership of England. No nobler game could be imagined. There was no nobler an aspiration if people honestly struggled to obtain it. So far as Mr. Chamberlain was concerned he believed the national verdict would be that he had played a solitary game for a personal honour. Mr. Chamberlain now preached imperialism but imperialism was not what he really meant. What had the volunteers

The Exa.

who went to South Africa to fight for the honour and glory of England, who fought as they believed to uphold the banner of the country, what had they to say? What share had they had and what share did Mr. Chamberlain otter them in the Imperialistic advance of the empire in South Africa? (Applause.) Who had got the benefit of Mr. Chamberlain's Imperialism ? The Jews of 'Johannesburg, the Jews of London, the money ringers, money lenders, and money changers who desecrated the temple in the days of old. They it was who reaped the rewards of British Imperialism. (Applause.) Mr. 1 Chamberlain was again trying to lead the people astray with his cry of Imperialism. The real Imperial- ism was that a Frenchman, Russian, German, or even an Irishman was not to be blamed for his nationality. The desire to do away with all armament and to submit to the decision of an international arbitrator was Imperial- ism in the right direction. It might be and was no doubt a dream in these days but he did not think it beyond the intelliegence of man to do all that. (Applause.) A vote of thanks to the Chairman, proposed by the Rev. J. S. Hughes and seconded by Ar..l. Longhand concluded the meeting.

PRESENTATION TO MR W. E. ATKIN.

Fifteen Years' Secretaryship of the Musical Society.

There was a pleasing little ceremony at the con- clusion of the usual rehearsal in connection with the Warrington Musical Society held in Cairo-street schools on Tuesday evening. Mr. W.}. Atkin recently resigned the hon. secretaryship of the Society after fifteen years' devoted service, and opportunity was taken to present him with a token of esteem and appreciation in the shape of a beautiful silver rose bowl and a cheque sub- 'scribed for by members of the Society and others inter- ested in its work. Mr. F. Monks made the presenta- tion in the presence of a large gathering. Before the speech-making took place, Mr. F. Crossley, conductor of the Society, read a letter from Mr. John White expressing, as an old member of the Society, his great appreciation of Mr. Atkin's services as hon. secretary for so many years and, for the most part, in face of great difficulties, financial and otherwise. Mr. 1110 ems said they were asseetbled to make some little recognition of the value of Mr. Atkins' services as secretary of the Society for fifteen years. He (Mr. Monks) sometimes thought it would be as well if they gave expression now and again to their appreciation of the services which were rendered without any hope of fee or reward. It would be an encouragement to the younger men if thanks were tendered to' them, not necessarily in a public manlier, for the work they did. He hardly thought we recognized sufficiently the amount of valuable public work that was done without any reward except the consciousness of having done one's duty in one's day and generation. It was a very proper thing that the Society should seek to give expresion in a formal way to their sense of the services , performed by Mr. Atkin (Applause). They would consider it flattery if he attempted to recount Mr. Atkin's work so ha would leave them to imagine what it was. Those who had to do with secretarial work to any extent knew how very much time and thought a man had to give to the direction of the affairs of any Society which was to be thoroughly prosperous. One thing he must congratulate the Society upon most heartily was the fact that they were no longer in financial difficulties. He was sure it had somewhat of a burden to many of their leaders to have to conduct the Society with a shortness of funds, and he was glad that state of affairs was past and gone. It would not be surprised if sometime when a balance had been struck, they found themselves in possession of a good round sum of at least £5OO. (Applause.) That would enable them to do a great deal. How much of their success was due to Mr. Atkin's exertions he was not in a position to say, but was quite sure their secretary had not left others to do the work while he took, as it were, the honour- able position. He was certain that he was expressing the feeling of everyone in the room when he said that the present was given with sincere wishes for Mr. Atkin's welfare. They hoped the business step he had taken would be for his material advantage and that he would not leave Warrington, but would continue to be connected with the Society for 1 many years. He had pleasure in asking Mr. Atkin's acceptance of the rose bowl and cheque from the mem- bers of the Society and many friends outside, with their heartiest good-will and best wishes for his welfare. (Applause). Mr. Monks then handed the gifts to Mr. Atkin. The rose bowl bears the inscription :—" Presented to Mr. W. E. Atkin by the members of the Musical Society on the occasion of his resignation of the secretaryship, as a mark of their appreciation and esteem. 1490-1903." Mr. ATKIN said he could not express his gratitude for such a handsome present, but he could assure them he thanked them from the bottom of his heart. The fifteen years during which he had had the great honour of being the secretary of the Society had not perhaps been years of quite unalloyed pleasure , because he believed every secretary would find at times that work of that kind was momentarily irksome. Any. thing he had done, however, had been amply repaid by the kindly feelngs always shown towards him by the whole of the members and the officials. He had not relinquished the duties through any wish to be relieved of the office. He would have liked to continue in it much longer but, as many of them knew, he had through business reasons to be out of Warrington all day. He hoped, however,to live in the town for a long time and to be able to take his part in the Society's work. The very handsome rose bowl would serve not to keep green the memory of those fifteen years but to m )ke those years appear brighter, because when be looked at it he would think of the great kindness and affection they had shown towards him. (Applause).
