SUICIDE OF MR. CHARLES HIOGINBOTTOM.

Tannery Secretary's Tragic End.

MM

A sensation wis occasioned in Warrington bv the news that Mr. Charles Higgiubottorn, Grappenhall road, Stockton Heath, secretary to Messrs. Arthur Waring and Co., Ltd.. tanners and buffalo hide and sizing merchants, Winwick-road, had committed suicide by hanging himself. The deceased who was widely known and highly respected had for some time suffered from depression of spirits. On Saturday night ho left his house and nothing further was seen of him until his body was found on Monday hanging from a beam in the tool-shed at the rear of the house. Some two years ago Mr. and Mrs. Iligginbottorn visited Harrogate for a holiday, and while shopping one morning Mrs. Higgin- bottom fall to the ground and became unconscious. She was picked up and taken to the hospital where she died. The sad event was a groat shock to her husband who has since suffered greatly from nervous depressson. Mr. Robert Dobson held the inquest on Tuesday after- noon at the Mulberry Tree Hotel, Stockton Heath, Mr. Jenkins represented the relatives of the deceased, and Mr. Willett dliss Turnock, the lady to whom, it is said, the deceased had recently become engaged. Frederick Ho ward,3B,Gra ppenhall-road,Stock ton Heath, said he was a groat friend of the deceased whom he saw on Sunday week, Deceased said he was not well and complained of his nerves. He slightly rambled in his speech. He was a gentleman of somewhat melancholic temperament. Eliza Margaret Bates, of 7, Carleton-street, said she was a friend of Mrs. Higginbottom (deceased's mother). Witness was at the deceased's house on Saturday. Mr. lligginbottorn seemed very strange; he had been very strange for a fortnight. Ele suffered a great deal from depression. He had never said anything which would lead to the supposition that he would take away his life. She last saw him alive in the dining-room at his house at four o'clock on Saturday afternoon. On Mon-. day afternoon she went into the garden when her atten tion WAS drawn to the door of the tool house being open. She weat in and se v the decessed hanging from a beam. She rushed away for the doctor and the police. Sergeant Barlow said he was called to Mr. Higgin- boitom's house on Monday afternoon. When he got to the tool shed he found Dr. Parker there and Mr. Howard. The body had bean suspended by the neck by a thin cord from the beam. Mr. Efigginbottom had every appearance of having been dead a couple of days. The Coroner, iu summing up, slid the deceased gentleman was much respected, and his death had been a great shock te all his friends. He had no doubt Mr. Higgin'eottom had committed suicide by hanging. He should not have pat it so plainly, but that there could be no two opinions about it. A juror (Mr. Dtliow) asked if the deceased left any- thing behind him. The Coroner said there was a letter of a very private character, but he had hoped the jury would have been able to come to a conclusion without referring to it. In the letter he said " May you and God forgive me this rash act." The jury returned a verdict that deceased committed suicide whilst of unsound raind. ! The Coroner and several jurors expressed their am In. pathy with the relatives, and Mr. Willett promised to convey these expressions.

er, Saturday, November 18, 1905.

THE ETHICS OF THE FISCAL QUESTION.

PAPER BY MR. C. E. PARKER.

The weekly meeting of the Warrington Junior Liberal Association was hold at Gregory's Card on Monday evening, when a piper on "The ethics of the fiscal question " was given by Mr. C. E. Parker, president of the senior Liberal Association.

Mr. J. HAWTHORN presided over a good attendance, and said that though they wereenly a young organisation they were hoping that the tr tieing which they. might be able to get would help them to take an amportaut position in the Liberal forces in the town. (Applause). Mr. Parker in his address, said one of the most striking features of the Fiscal controversy was the fact that it had been almost entirely limited to the .C. s. d. aspect of the question. Seldom had the disputants on either side risen above the purely material. At the base as well as at the summit of our economics, were moral and intellectual principles,which could not be ignored with impunity. As an evidence of the relation between the material, the mental and moral we found striking similarity of laws governing these different spheres—laws,which ran parallel to each other. A similarity of law so striking that it Was more accurate to say that there was an identity of law runn- ing not parallel but vertically by a process of projection through all the spheres of being, demanding obedience alike an the higher as in the lower, and in the lower as in the higher. In view of this essential unity it was ' worth while examining the Fiscal question in the clear light of the intellectual and moral spheres. Minds limited in their build and range by the purely economic could by a deductive logical process of reasoning prove that restrictions, (miscalled Protection) were injurious and nnwise from the material side, but such reasoning lacked the potentiality derived from moral considera- tions. It was the ethical which approved or condemned with a finality from which there was no appeal, any course of human action. It had bee's finely said " that it was the Chrstian and holy ethic which had the rever- sion of the long economic future." To adapt Emerson's , figure we mist hitch the wagon to the stars. No apology was needed for testing present day economic theoriea by this standard. Those who resented the introduction of moral considerations into the political and coup mercial world put aside the most potent force in human evolution. The history of the developmeat of the race showed very clearly that moral forces had been the most powerful in the evolutionary processes. This application of the ethical test to current fiscal pro- posals would be unpopular. The present dominance of reactionary ideas (of which Protection was the latest evidence) made unpalatable an apdeal to ethical con- sideration. A tide of materialism was beating with perilous impact upon the country's noblest moral tradition. Still notwithstanding l the vulgarisation of national ideals which had of late lowered the nation's morale, the conviction that national policy must have its foundation in morality was too deeply rooted in the English character to ha permanently affected by temporary reaction. The issue would be determined by the answer to the. question, "Is it right? " even although the answer conflicted with that of "Will it pay? ' What then vere the moral effects of Pro- , tection ? It washy the fruit that we knew the tree.

THE EFFECT ON PARLIAMENT AND POLITICS. The public life of England in spite of all its faults was as pure if not purer, than that of any country in I the world. Who could doubt this was largely due to the fact, that England had been wise enough and great enough to adopt and adhere to Free Trade. By this means she had eliminated the temptation, to which the politicians of all prhtected countries had succumbed. The temptation to sacrifice the general welfare to the gain, at the people's expense,of industries in which they were interested. Turn our Parliament into a permanent Tariff Revision Committee, and we would at once make it the resort of the representatives of decrepit or selfish industries, who would find it pro- fitable to spend their time and money in seeking to maintain and increase the tariffs by persuading or intimidating their representatives to vote for such maintenance or increase. Instead of elections being fought on broad national, moral and social issues, we would find every manufacturing constituency putting in the forefront of its programme Protection to their particular industries. The hope of protection for cement decided the election at Rochester in opposition to the general trend of national feeling. THE EFFECT ON LABOUR IDEALS.

- This tendency would not be confined to Members of Parliament, capitalists and employers, but the solidarity of the workers interests would be cleft to splinters by the same sordid considerations.Every't r tdewould fight for its own agaiest every other and make common labour interests a secondary consideration. We would have the hope of individual gain conflicting with the ethical duty of seeking the greatest good for the greatest number. We would have private selfishness bidding against the well-being of the commonwealth. In the present state of moral evolution, and the hard lot of many who toiled,. we would certainly see the choice of apparent gain to 3ndividual local interests ousting the claims of the welfare of the community. From the effect of this atmosphere of conflicting class interests, and corrupt political tendency might the moral life of the country be saved. Happily the great bulk of the associated workers recognised the wedge Protection become in splitting their numbers into antagonistic sections, and would have none of it. If unhappily the cluntry was hypnotized into the adoption of Protection under whatever guise social and political progress would receive a staggering blow from which it would take a long time to recover. If Protection became the basis of our fiscal system then advancement along moral and social lines would be heavily handicapped by the conflict between duty and self interest. For these reasons all who desired to see the nation and the Empira progress must fight Protection to the death.

TUG EFFECT ON LAW AND MORALrTY. It was universally acknowledged that there was no country in the world in which law was more respected and obeyed than in our own. We did not claim that freedom from Protection was the only Cause of this, but undoubtedly protective tariffs were among the most effective causes which undermined respect for law. Protection created arbitrary differences, penalties, and privileges against which self interest and justice alike rebelled. There was no surer way of bringing law into disrepute than to award punishment for attempts to evade laws which had no solid foundation in equity. Hence we had in connection with the custom houses in everyprotectei country an atmosphere of fraud and corruption, There were the endless devices to get behind the tariff, false decorations' bribery of officials, adulteration to change the character of the goods, and make them to look other than they are. All these , tended to blurr the moral sense, to weaken the con- , ception which ought ever to exist in connection with law—that it derived its authority from the principles of right and equity, and was not as Was too often the case of tariffs, the result of the struggle of powerful interests, and the jockeying of political parties. SELF SUFFERING AND ISOLATION. Let them now examine the general trend of the teach- ings associated with the term Fiscal Reform, as put before the country by Mr. Chamberlain. This was dis- tinctly in the direction of national or rather imperial I self snfficiency and isolation. It aimed at a self con- tained empire. To attain this end encouragement by preference of exclaance in certain arbitiaeity selected products was to be given to the several communities ' owning allegiance to our crown ; and on the other hand intercourse and trade between the rest of the world I was to be discouraged by means of fiscal barriers at all the entrances to the Empire. How did this policy I appear from intellectual and ethical standpoints. In the spheres of science, art, literature, philosophy and religion we were accustomed to reoognise that the freest interaourse made for universal progress, for the advancement of the race as a whole, and of each of its parts. Whoever heard of a movement to protect us from the science of America's Edison, the art of Ttaly's Michml Angelo, the writing of Frances Victor Hugo ? And yet why not ? If it was well to discourage the , product of the labour of the head and hand of those in , other countries who converted the bounty which nature lavished upon their lands into food and clothing, and all other things we required, why should we welcome the richer products of the minds of other lands which ' reached upwards towards the highest and the best ? Were we to extend the argument of modern reformers to the higher developments of the race and say " we will not have the music of Germany, the art of Italy, and the literature of France dumped into this country and her colonies. We will discourage these so as to give British and Colonial musicians, artists, and writers a I chance. Let these foreigners keep their music,their great thoughts, their art to themselves and England toe the English be henceforth our motto. Let us develop our own philosophy, art and literature by n system of exclusive home culture." Now it was possible that Tariff Reformers would seek to escape from the applica- tion of their own doctrines when applied to these things, on the ground that such were removed from the sphere of their application, because they had no definite money value, and argue that there was an essential difference because the lower things were aglutinatecl by cash nexus? But wherein lay the difference? Were the principles, the laws, which resulted in mutual benefit, through a free interchange of the hest products of the mind inapplicable or were reversed when applied to more material things? Did not the same principles, the same laws, project downwards and work as beneficently in the lower spheres as in the higher? in one Case we claimed as members of the human race free access to the world's best thought, as a necessity for our fullest development, a right which was nowhere denied, in the other we claimed the right of free exchange in service or goods. Why should the element of exchange introduce a different principle into the question? The advantage of this exchange arose first out of the fact that there were in other lands certain differences of soil and climate, producing more readily than we could, certain products which we desired and secondly in the human family there was a variety of human adaptability. Brain power, technique, thesemade an interchange of the resulting prodncts profitable. He dismissed the economic question which might arise in some minds as to whether this interchange was profitable to a country in dealing with natiens which have erected barriers against the products we would exchange. He dismissed

this aspect of the question, first because it was outside the purpose of the paper, and would lead away from the ethical point of view, and second because the economic difficulty was too elementary to require explanation at that advanced stage of the controversy. By freedom of exchange the greatest material as well as intellectual advantage was secured. This advantage, though of ten curtailed by countries adopting restrictive methods, still rested with the country which placed no barrier to free exchange. The advantage of exchange explained the efforts which were continually being made to facilitate communication between different sections of the human race. We built ships, we built rivers, we tunnelled mountains, all to bring the produce of the earth and its people together. Having done all this, having cleared away the physical barriers, it was para- doxical to erect fiscal barriers, the effect of which was to render less effective the work of those wha by vast expenditure of toil and treasure had removed natural y impediments to a free arid easy exchange. Oae instance ! illustrating this might be given in connection with the ' cutting of the Mont Cenis tunnel. As soon as this was opened for traffic, and France and Italy exchanged their products through it. a movement on both sides of the' tunnel was coma:termed by the inhabitants fer the pur- pose of the institution of a tariff to prevent this inter- change which the tunnel had made easy, alid for which indeed it Was beilt. Bind communities together by the thousand ties of mutual interest, which International trade develops, and they created a healthy . sentiment which was ever on the side of a peaceful settlement of international difficulties. The essential unity of the race, the brotherhood of man, bsth called for the removal of every obstacle which kept apart the members of the great family of nations. The human race reached its highest by the freest innoculation among its many members. Without this innoculation the pro- gress of the race would be ret trded, with it the race would advance with a cumulative power inherent in all the interdependent processes of nature. (Applause) They saw then that the proposals of the Protectionist school aiming at isolation, limiting the opportunities of asseciation, conflicted with a permanent and necessary factor in human development, with the pessfbility of all entering into the full enjoyment of the best the world had to offer, whether that best ministered to mental, moral or material well-being. In so far then , the proposals were retrograde, the tollgates they sought to sot up across the road of hu:nan progress ware resusi- talons of raedeval conceptions which ought to have passed away with the curfew, the pillory and the stocks. Protection was repugnant to justice because it set up artificial and tattiest inequalities and enabled one olass to invade the rights of others. It paralysed political purity because it turned the power of law making into a means of enriching some at the expense of others. It was both the offspring and the parent of corruption. It era Lted and encouraged imonopoly. It lowered the power of wages. Commercially it was unwise, economically it was unsound and wasteful, and ethically it was unjust. Let it. be the part of those here who represented, the youthful and virile forces of Liberalism not only to maintain but also to extend the freedom of thought and of exchange with which names of Cobden and Bright. Peel and Gladstonekere inseparably'essociated (A pplanse) Mr. Hawrisoest said that if there was one advantage which had accrued to us as a nation from the discussion of Mr. Chamberlain's proposals it was that we had been led to think more about politics and economics. The ethical was one of the aspects of the question which had not been brought forward so much as the bread and butter side of the question. Mr. Chamberlain in one of his epigramatic sentences said "Protection would be unwise even if it were possible and would be impossible even if it were wise." The moment we gave any tariff preference to any particular trades, manufacturers would get into Parliament to look after their own particular interests. (Hear, hear.) It would be necessary for them to do so for their own self protection, and we would see conflicting interests fighting each other- for their own pecuniary advantage. After 60 years of Free Trar,e we were not going to revert beck to the bad old days of Protection and to a system which had, in America and other countries lei to jobbery and cor- ruption. (Applause). Mr. FORD moved a vote of thanks to Mr. Parker for his address. .

Mr. H. GRAY seconded. Protection, he said, was one of the evils which prevented the solidarity of the human family. One of the demands of the times was that young men should view the various political questions from the moral standpoint. (Hear, hear.) The vote was heartily accorded. A vote of thanks to the Chairman concluded the meeting.

NOTES ON GAMES AND ATHLETICS. • Rugby Football. [By " Ovalist";

Warrington have no match to-day. They should have gone to Hanalei, but the Yorkshiremen are among the semi-finalists for the Yorkshire Senior Cup and play Hull Kingston Rovers at Hull. The War- rington men therefore take a rest, which should do them good and bring the return of the injured " cracks " nearer realisation. It is a practical certainty that they would hive been well-beaten at Hunslet, and there is a chance now that a stronger side may be available when the fixture is re-arranged. The " A " team will occupy the boards at Waders- pool and may be relied upon to give Salford Adelphi a useful lesson. By the way this is not. a Combina- tion match. (-Lying to the disbandment of the Tyldosley Club, there are now only eight clubs which are members of the Lancashire Combination.

The semi-finalists for the Lancashire Senior Q,up also play off to-day, on the grounds of the first-named clubs. The arrangements are as follow : Oldham v. Wigan, J. Oakland ; T. Brimelow and J. 11'Gowan. _ _

-- Leigh v. Widnes, F. Renton; W. Jones, and EL Lowe.

Last Saturday the Warrington man had the extreme felicity of going on the field conscious of the \fact that they were certain to win if they tried. They have not been in this position very often this season, and the absence of anxiety about the result would probably be a pleasant change. Rochdale Hornets brought a better team than the one which opposed Warrington in the first round of the Lancashire Cup, but they were far from being equal to the class of the cup-holders, depleted though tho latter's forces have been. Only a small crowd assembled, but they were rewarded by an interesting, though not an exciting, match. It must be said to the credit of the visitors that they persevered, played the open game, and to* their defeat by 19 8 in very good part. •

Warrington had a big superiority in forward strength, but they did not make full use of it, 'and I should say that very few of the pushers pushed with real earnestness for more than a few minutes at a time. Consequently the Hornets got the ball quite frequently and did their best to emulate the best methods of back play. They handled the leather with considerable facility, but like the Barrow men, they were seldom dangerous and Lacked finishing pnwor. The two tries they got, and which made the score level at half-time were really due to Warrington's carelessness, though Blincow made the most of both opportunities very smartly. Had it been a cup-tie I don't think the Hornets would have got within speak- ing distance of the home goal-line,

The Warrington men played with that success begot by conadeime. Quite early on a fine round of passing resulted in Isherwood making an opening for Knight, and the " A " team skipper ran so well as to com- plete the effort behind the posts. Then Hockenhall obliged with one of his rare, but brilliaat corkscrew runs which led up to a goal by Preston. Brookes got another try, by clever dribbang, and the Hornets also complied 8 points prior to the interval. They may have thought that their luck was going to turn and a viotory, crown their efforts at last. The hope was shortrlived for on resumption Harris and Knight quickly added tries, air! Brooks also got over agsin before the elose,the Hornets being quite overpowered. S.) far they have played tan ra itches with never a win, hut they will improve on such a doleful record ere long, I am sure.

Mather-lane folk a fright when they held the lead up to threequarter time. Leeds have now gone to the head of affairs with 8 wins and one defeat in 9 matches. Oldham's ambitions received a check at Salford, but the Rangers managed to get a little sweet revenge at Wigan. Meanwhile Warrington seem to have taken a long lease of their old position at half way, with a percentage of 50 and exactly a point per match.

Hockey.

For their match with Altrincharn on the Arpley ground Stockton Heath were minus several players, although they managed to put a good side into the field. The ground was very slippery, in fact on one occasion the Altrinlham goal must have fallen, but for the fact that W. H. Banks failed to turn, owing to the slippery ground.

Both sides showed good hockey, while one had an opportunity of contrasting the long passing with the short passing game. Without a doubt the long passing game is the game to play when in mid-field,this was clearly shown by the way Altrincharn made gronnd, their sweeping attacks being very effective. The fault the visitors tnAde, was that even after passing the Stockton 25 line, they kept the ball too much on the wings. Had they done otherwise a different tale would have to be told. As it was the home forwards were always dangerous near goal, and although a draw might have been a better result on the day's play, this deadli- ness near goal made the home side perhaps a shade the better team.

W. Richardson attain showed line form when near goal. The two points he obtained on Saturday were beautiful shots, while his second was scored about two minutes from time. This was the winning goal, and if it had been missed, a draw would without doubt have been the result.

It is lucky for Stockton Heath that H. Kirkconnel decided to play again this season. He is playing a fine game, and on Saturday was about the best man on the field. He had a big snare in Stockton Heath gaining the day by 3-2.

L. Fairclough has been chosen reserve right full-back for Cheshire v. Lincoln on November 18th. He is quite worth his place, and should he not be required to-dly,it cannot be long before the county authorities will call upon him again.

For their return match with Latchford to-dqv at Arpley Stockton Heath will lack the services of E. E. Woods, Eric and Lionel Fairclough, but R. Pierpoint will turn out, and his presence among the forwards should make a big difference.

First team :—R. G. Pierpoint goal ; E. Pierpoint and P. B. Flawtin, backs ; E. R. Jones, H. Kirkconnel and J. G. Hatton, halves ; S. Baxter, R. Pierpoint, W. H. Banks, W. Richardson, and T. Day, forwards. Bully-off, 2.45. Second team :—F. G. Kertland, goal ; Egerton Pair- dough and B. Silcock, backs; A. Hodgkinson, H. Smith, and J. Oliff, halves ; W. Tinnell, R. Dolan, W. Howard. H. Owen, T. Pierpoint, forwards. Referee, N. W Bellamy.

Latchford mot South Manchester on Saturday last, and the match resulted in a win for the visitors by 4 goals to nil. It is only fair to Latchford to say this score hardly represents the play, as, although on the day's play the best team won, the margin ought not to hays been so great. Both teams played a fast combined game, but Latchford lacked finish, whereas South rarely missed an opportunity of aeleast attempting to score.

Latchford" A" played 'Winnington Park at Winning- ton on Saturday last. The ground proved to be rather heavy. The home forwards combined well, and before time was called had scored two goala. The visitors were somewhat weak in this respect, and were over- played, a very pleasant game resulting in a win for Winnington by 2 goals to nil.

The teams for to-day's return with Stockton Heath are :—First Eleven (at Warrington) : Goal, W. Unsworth ; full banks, Et. Hallows and T. Dixon; half hacks, W. Knight. J. B. Wilkinson, and A. H. Rath- hone ;forwards, WIFf. Spensley R. L. Jones,D. Knight Cliffe, and J. Callon.

Second Eleven (at Latchford) : W. &mbar, goal : C. Hale and T. Yonng, backs; W. B. Cobb, H. Heald and D. Travis, half-backs; forwards, W. B. Smith, H. Warburton, A. Allender, W. H. Pickles, and J. Wilkin- son.

Stockton Heath visited Sale and enjoyed a fast and exciting bvame. For the visitors GibSs ,played a fair game at full back although not playing up to his usual standard. The threequartersplayed a hard game, A. V. Ferguson in particular doing a lot of good work. H. Davenport and A. Bradshaw at half did well, Brad- shaw however still shows a tendency to stick too long to the ball instead of passing out to his threes. The forwards did not play up to the previous Saturday's form and did not settle down until the closing stages of the game when they kept Sale in their own 25 by good work both in the scrums and the loose. Cooke was the pick of the forwards but Gibbs, Owen and Barker played well. For Sale the forwards played well, beat- ing the visitors pack for possession the first half and quite holding their own in the second except for 15 minutes at the close of the game. The backs combined much better than the visitors although individually they were in no way superior.

Christ Church. -- -- FRIENDLY MATCH. WARRINGTON ALBIONS V. SALFORD UNITED—As special attraction at the Liverpool-road enclosnre on Saturda. Warrington Albions arranged a match with Salford United, who are members of the Manchester 'League. There was a record gate. Owing to unfore- seen circumstances, Salord were only able to send a reservelßeami but a good game was witnessed. Mr. A. H. Crasfield kicked off. The Albions held the upper hanchhroughout, and ran out winners by six goals to nil. The scorers were Houghton (2), Dutton (3), Ball 1(1).
